<a id='question-074'></a>

### Question 074: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 24 (Phagocytosis)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanisms of phagocytosis, including opsonization, receptor-mediated uptake, phagosome-lysosome fusion, and the role of intracellular killing mechanisms (oxidative burst, reactive oxygen species) in eliminating ingested pathogens. (理解吞噬作用的机制，包括调理作用、受体介导的摄取、吞噬体-溶酶体融合以及胞内杀伤机制（氧化爆发、活性氧）在清除摄入的病原体中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic granulomatous disease (CGD) presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, chills, and productive cough with purulent sputum. He reports recent exposure to a neighbor's dog. Physical examination reveals a temperature of 39.5°C, tachypnea, and crackles in the right lower lobe. Chest X-ray shows a right lower lobe consolidation suggestive of pneumonia. Sputum Gram stain reveals numerous extracellular and intracellular Gram-positive cocci in clusters, consistent with *Staphylococcus aureus*. Blood cultures are pending. The patient's history of CGD is significant because it impairs the ability of phagocytes to kill certain ingested microorganisms. Which of the following mechanisms is most directly impaired in this patient, leading to recurrent and severe infections with catalase-positive organisms like *S. aureus*?

(A) Complement-mediated opsonization of the pathogen.
(B) Phagosome-lysosome fusion.
(C) Production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) within the phagolysosome.
(D) Antigen presentation via MHC class II molecules on the phagocyte surface.
(E) Chemotaxis of neutrophils to the site of infection.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with chronic granulomatous disease (CGD). This immediately points towards a defect in phagocyte function, specifically intracellular killing.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Fever, chills, productive cough with purulent sputum, tachypnea, crackles, right lower lobe consolidation – classic signs and symptoms of pneumonia.
- **Exposure History:** Recent exposure to a dog, suggesting a possible zoonotic source or environmental exposure.
- **Microbiological Findings:** Sputum Gram stain shows *Staphylococcus aureus* (Gram-positive cocci in clusters), both extracellularly and intracellularly. The intracellular location is key, indicating phagocytosis has occurred.
- **Pathophysiology Link:** CGD is characterized by impaired intracellular killing of phagocytosed microbes, particularly catalase-positive organisms like *S. aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, and *Aspergillus* species.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most directly impaired mechanism* in CGD leading to severe infections with *S. aureus*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** CGD is a primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in genes encoding subunits of the NADPH oxidase enzyme complex (e.g., *CYBB* for X-linked CGD, *CYBA*, *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4* for autosomal recessive CGD).
- **NADPH Oxidase Function:** The NADPH oxidase complex is crucial for the "respiratory burst" or "oxidative burst" in phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages). This complex transfers electrons from NADPH in the cytoplasm to molecular oxygen (O2) in the phagolysosome, generating superoxide anion (O2•−).
- **Reactive Oxygen Species (ROS) Production:** Superoxide anion is rapidly converted to other ROS, including hydrogen peroxide (H2O2), hydroxyl radical (•OH), and hypochlorous acid (HOCl, via myeloperoxidase). These ROS are highly toxic to ingested microbes and are essential for their killing.
- **CGD Pathophysiology:** In CGD, the defective NADPH oxidase cannot produce sufficient ROS. Phagocytes can engulf microbes (phagocytosis occurs), and phagosome-lysosome fusion also occurs normally. However, the lack of ROS prevents effective killing of the ingested catalase-positive organisms. Catalase-positive organisms break down H2O2 into water and oxygen, further hindering the killing process if H2O2 is the primary ROS.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Recurrent, severe bacterial and fungal infections, often leading to granuloma formation (hence the name "granulomatous disease") due to the inability to clear microbes effectively. Common pathogens include *S. aureus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia*, *Aspergillus*, and *Nocardia*.

##### 3. Evaluation of Options
- **(A) Complement-mediated opsonization:** Opsonization (coating pathogens with complement or antibodies) enhances phagocytosis but is not the primary defect in CGD. While opsonization is important, the killing step is impaired.
- **(B) Phagosome-lysosome fusion:** This process, where the phagosome containing the microbe fuses with a lysosome containing digestive enzymes, is generally intact in CGD. The defect lies in the killing mechanisms *within* the phagolysosome.
- **(C) Production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) within the phagolysosome:** This is the hallmark defect in CGD. The inability of the NADPH oxidase complex to generate ROS directly impairs the killing of ingested microbes, especially catalase-positive ones. This aligns perfectly with the patient's history and the causative organism.
- **(D) Antigen presentation via MHC class II molecules:** Phagocytes (especially macrophages and dendritic cells) present antigens via MHC class II to activate T helper cells. This process is not directly affected by the NADPH oxidase defect in CGD.
- **(E) Chemotaxis of neutrophils:** Chemotaxis is the directed migration of phagocytes towards the site of infection. While some studies suggest minor chemotactic defects in certain CGD subtypes, the primary and defining defect is the lack of ROS production for intracellular killing.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Complement-mediated opsonization of the pathogen:** Opsonization enhances phagocytosis but doesn't explain the *killing* defect characteristic of CGD. Patients with CGD can still opsonize pathogens, but the phagocytes cannot effectively kill them. This option confuses the enhancement of uptake with the mechanism of killing.
- **(B) Phagosome-lysosome fusion:** This step is necessary for delivering lysosomal enzymes to the microbe. While crucial for killing, it is generally functional in CGD. Defects in phagosome-lysosome fusion are seen in other immunodeficiencies, like Chediak-Higashi syndrome. This option targets a different step in the phagocytic process.
- **(D) Antigen presentation via MHC class II molecules on the phagocyte surface:** Antigen presentation is a separate function of phagocytes, crucial for adaptive immunity. It is not directly linked to the NADPH oxidase defect and the inability to produce ROS. This option confuses intracellular killing with antigen presentation.
- **(E) Chemotaxis of neutrophils to the site of infection:** Chemotaxis is the process of neutrophils migrating to the infection site. While impaired chemotaxis can occur in some immunodeficiencies (e.g., Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency), it is not the primary defect in CGD. The main problem in CGD is the failure to kill microbes *after* they have been phagocytosed and reached the site. This option targets a different aspect of the inflammatory response.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | Chediak-Higashi Syndrome | Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (LAD) | Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :--------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH oxidase, ROS production       | Lysosomal trafficking    | Integrin expression (β2 subunit)   | T and B cell development              |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | Phagocytes, NK cells, etc. | Neutrophils                       | Lymphocytes (T, B, NK)                |
| **Key Manifestation**   | Recurrent bacterial/fungal infections, granulomas | Recurrent infections, partial oculocutaneous albinism, neurological issues | Recurrent bacterial infections, delayed umbilical cord separation, impaired wound healing | Severe recurrent infections (viral, bacterial, fungal, opportunistic) |
| **Phagocytosis**        | Normal uptake                       | Impaired uptake & killing | Normal uptake, impaired migration | Normal (but lack of adaptive immunity) |
| **Intracellular Killing** | Impaired (ROS dependent)            | Impaired (lysosomal fusion) | Impaired (cannot reach site)       | N/A (primarily adaptive immunity)      |
| **Common Pathogens**    | Catalase+ bacteria, *Aspergillus*   | *S. aureus*, *S. pyogenes* | *S. aureus*, *E. coli*             | All types                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CGD is a defect in the NADPH oxidase system, leading to impaired production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) necessary for intracellular killing of phagocytosed microbes, particularly catalase-positive organisms. Key differentials include other phagocytic defects like Chediak-Higashi syndrome (lysosomal fusion defect) and Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (chemotaxis/adhesion defect). Remember that phagocytosis itself (uptake) and phagosome-lysosome fusion are generally intact in CGD.

<a id='question-075'></a>

### Question 075: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 25 (Figure)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) in recognizing and eliminating intracellular pathogens, specifically viruses, via MHC class I presentation and the release of cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes, leading to target cell apoptosis. (理解CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞(CTLs)在识别和清除细胞内病原体，特别是病毒的作用，通过MHC I类分子呈递和释放含有穿孔素和颗粒酶的细胞毒性颗粒，导致靶细胞凋亡。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, myalgia, headache, and a diffuse maculopapular rash. He reports recent travel to Southeast Asia for a 2-week volunteer trip, where he participated in outdoor activities, including hiking and camping. He denies any known tick bites or mosquito bites but recalls being bitten by an unknown insect. Physical examination reveals a temperature of 38.5°C, generalized lymphadenopathy, and the characteristic rash. Laboratory tests show leukopenia and elevated liver enzymes. A skin biopsy from the rash reveals lymphocytes infiltrating the epidermis and dermis, with some lymphocytes showing cytolytic activity against keratinocytes. Further investigation reveals the presence of viral inclusion bodies within infected cells. The patient's serum is negative for IgM and IgG antibodies against common arboviruses like Dengue, Zika, and Chikungunya. However, flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in the proportion of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed cytolytic activity of lymphocytes against infected keratinocytes in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing Fc receptors on infected keratinocytes.
(B) Complement-mediated lysis of infected keratinocytes via the membrane attack complex (MAC).
(C) CD4+ T helper cells activating macrophages to phagocytose infected keratinocytes.
(D) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing viral peptides presented on MHC class I molecules of infected keratinocytes and inducing apoptosis.
(E) Neutrophils releasing reactive oxygen species (ROS) and proteolytic enzymes to degrade infected keratinocytes.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Young adult male with fever, myalgia, headache, rash after travel to Southeast Asia. This suggests an infectious etiology, possibly vector-borne given the travel history and outdoor exposure.
- **Physical Exam:** Fever, lymphadenopathy, maculopapular rash. These are non-specific signs of infection, but the rash is a key feature.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Leukopenia (low white blood cell count) and elevated liver enzymes are common in viral infections.
- **Skin Biopsy:** Lymphocytes infiltrating the epidermis/dermis with cytolytic activity against keratinocytes. This points towards a cell-mediated immune response targeting infected skin cells. The presence of viral inclusion bodies confirms an intracellular viral infection.
- **Serology:** Negative for common arboviruses (Dengue, Zika, Chikungunya). This helps narrow down the differential diagnosis.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin. This strongly indicates activation of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs), which are key mediators of cell-mediated immunity against intracellular pathogens like viruses.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for the cytolytic activity against infected keratinocytes.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Cell-Mediated Immunity:** The vignette describes a scenario where the immune system is targeting virus-infected host cells. This is the hallmark of cell-mediated immunity, primarily driven by T lymphocytes.
- **CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs):** These cells are crucial for eliminating intracellular pathogens, particularly viruses and intracellular bacteria. They recognize pathogen-derived peptides presented on MHC class I molecules, which are expressed on virtually all nucleated cells, including keratinocytes.
- **MHC Class I Presentation:** Infected cells process viral proteins into peptides and load them onto MHC class I molecules in the endoplasmic reticulum. These MHC I-peptide complexes are then transported to the cell surface.
- **CTL Activation and Killing:** CD8+ T cells recognize the MHC I-peptide complex via their T cell receptor (TCR). This recognition, along with co-stimulatory signals, activates the CTL. Activated CTLs form an immunological synapse with the target cell and release cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes.
- **Perforin and Granzymes:** Perforin polymerizes on the target cell membrane, forming pores. Granzymes are serine proteases that enter the target cell through these pores and activate caspases, initiating the apoptotic pathway, leading to programmed cell death of the infected cell.
- **Flow Cytometry Confirmation:** The finding of increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin directly supports the involvement of CTLs in the immune response and their mechanism of action.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The clinical picture (fever, rash, travel history, leukopenia, elevated liver enzymes, viral inclusion bodies) is suggestive of several viral infections, including Dengue fever (though serology was negative), Chikungunya, or potentially other arboviruses endemic to Southeast Asia. Regardless of the specific virus, the immune response against infected keratinocytes involves CTLs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells:** ADCC involves antibodies binding to target cells, which are then recognized by Fc receptors on effector cells like NK cells or macrophages. While NK cells can kill virus-infected cells, the primary mechanism described in the vignette (lymphocytes infiltrating skin, CTL activation markers) points towards T cell-mediated cytotoxicity, not ADCC. Furthermore, ADCC typically requires pre-existing antibodies, and the patient's serology was negative for common arboviruses. NK cells primarily recognize cells lacking MHC class I expression or expressing stress ligands, not typically via ADCC against keratinocytes in this context.
- (B) **Complement-mediated lysis:** The complement system can lyse pathogens or infected cells, but it primarily targets extracellular pathogens or cells coated with antibodies (classical pathway) or expressing certain surface molecules (alternative/lectin pathways). While complement can be activated during viral infections, it is not the primary mechanism for eliminating virus-infected host cells, especially keratinocytes, which are not typically targets for complement-mediated lysis in this manner. CTL-mediated apoptosis is the dominant mechanism for clearing intracellular viral infections.
- (C) **CD4+ T helper cells activating macrophages:** CD4+ T helper cells (specifically Th1 cells) play a crucial role in activating macrophages to kill intracellular pathogens, particularly bacteria and parasites. Macrophages are phagocytic cells. While CD4+ T cells are involved in coordinating the immune response, the direct cytolytic activity against keratinocytes described in the biopsy is characteristic of CTLs (CD8+ T cells), not macrophages activated by CD4+ T cells.
- (E) **Neutrophils releasing ROS and proteolytic enzymes:** Neutrophils are primarily involved in the innate immune response against extracellular bacteria and fungi. They phagocytose pathogens and release reactive oxygen species (ROS) and proteolytic enzymes. While neutrophils can be recruited to sites of infection, they are not the primary mediators of cell-mediated immunity against intracellular viruses, nor are they typically responsible for the specific cytolytic activity against infected keratinocytes observed in the skin biopsy. The vignette highlights a lymphocyte-mediated process.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (D) - CTL Killing | Distractor (A) - ADCC/NK Cells | Distractor (B) - Complement | Distractor (C) - Th1/Macrophage | Distractor (E) - Neutrophils |
|--------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Immune Cell**          | CD8+ T Lymphocyte (CTL)           | NK Cell                         | N/A                         | CD4+ T Helper, Macrophage      | Neutrophil                  |
| **Target Cell**          | Virus-infected host cell (e.g., keratinocyte) | Antibody-coated target cell | Pathogen/Target cell        | Phagocytosed pathogen/cell    | Extracellular pathogen      |
| **Recognition Mechanism** | TCR-MHC Class I-peptide          | Fc Receptor-Antibody            | Complement components      | Cytokines-Macrophage receptors | Phagocytosis, PRRs          |
| **Effector Mechanism**   | Perforin/Granzyme-induced apoptosis | Perforin/Granzyme, Antibody     | Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) | Phagocytosis, ROS, Enzymes     | Phagocytosis, ROS, Enzymes     |
| **Pathogen Type**        | Intracellular (e.g., virus)        | Antibody-coated targets         | Extracellular/Coated cells  | Intracellular (bacteria/parasites) | Extracellular (bacteria/fungi)|
| **Key Molecules**        | MHC Class I, TCR, Perforin, Granzyme | Fc Receptor, Perforin, Granzyme | C3b, C5b-9                  | Cytokines (IFN-γ), TCR, MHC II  | PRRs, ROS, Proteases        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cell-mediated immunity, particularly via CD8+ CTLs, is the primary mechanism for eliminating virus-infected host cells. CTLs recognize viral peptides presented on MHC class I molecules and induce apoptosis through perforin and granzymes. This contrasts with antibody-mediated mechanisms (ADCC, complement) and phagocytosis (macrophages, neutrophils), which are less effective against intracellular viruses residing within host cells. Key differentials include NK cell activity (recognizing missing MHC I or stress ligands) and macrophage activation (primarily for intracellular bacteria/parasites).

<a id='question-076'></a>

### Question 076: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 26 (Mediated)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) in recognizing and eliminating intracellular pathogens, specifically viruses, via MHC class I presentation and the release of cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes, leading to target cell apoptosis. (理解CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞(CTLs)在识别和清除细胞内病原体，特别是病毒的作用，通过MHC I类呈递和释放含有穿孔素和颗粒酶的细胞毒性颗粒，导致靶细胞凋亡。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male software engineer presents to his primary care physician with a 3-week history of worsening fatigue, myalgia, and low-grade fever (37.8°C). He reports no recent travel, sick contacts, or specific exposures, but mentions increased stress at work. His physical examination is unremarkable except for mild cervical lymphadenopathy. Initial laboratory tests show elevated liver enzymes (AST 3x ULN, ALT 4x ULN) and mild thrombocytopenia (platelets 120,000/µL). A viral panel is ordered, and testing reveals acute infection with Epstein-Barr virus (EBV). One week later, the patient develops a maculopapular rash across his trunk and proximal extremities, which is non-pruritic. Further investigation reveals the presence of atypical lymphocytes (Downey cells) in his peripheral blood smear. Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST crucial for controlling the spread of EBV infection in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing EBV-infected B cells coated with IgG.
(B) Complement-mediated lysis of EBV-infected B cells via the classical pathway activation by viral surface proteins.
(C) Phagocytosis of apoptotic EBV-infected B cells by macrophages following CTL-mediated killing.
(D) Production of neutralizing antibodies by plasma cells that prevent EBV from entering uninfected B cells.
(E) Activation of CD4+ T helper cells that secrete IL-2, promoting B cell proliferation and antibody production against EBV.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with fatigue, myalgia, fever, lymphadenopathy, elevated liver enzymes, thrombocytopenia, and a maculopapular rash. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of infectious mononucleosis (IM).
- **Etiology:** Acute EBV infection is confirmed. EBV primarily infects B lymphocytes.
- **Pathophysiology:** EBV infection leads to significant B cell proliferation. The immune system mounts a response, primarily involving CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and CD4+ T helper cells. The atypical lymphocytes (Downey cells) seen in the peripheral blood smear are activated T lymphocytes responding to the infection.
- **Question Stem:** The question asks for the MOST crucial cellular mechanism for controlling the spread of EBV infection.
- **Key Clue:** EBV infects B cells intracellularly. Control of intracellular pathogens, especially viruses, relies heavily on cell-mediated immunity, specifically CTLs.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **EBV Pathogenesis:** EBV infects B cells via the CD21 receptor (complement receptor 2). Inside the B cell, EBV establishes latency, primarily expressing latent membrane proteins (LMPs) and latent nuclear antigens (LNAs). These proteins can contribute to B cell immortalization and transformation, potentially leading to Burkitt lymphoma or nasopharyngeal carcinoma.
- **Immune Response to EBV:**
    - **Innate Immunity:** Interferons (IFNs) play an early role. NK cells can kill infected cells, especially those that have downregulated MHC class I expression.
    - **Adaptive Immunity:**
        - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize EBV antigens presented on MHC class II molecules by B cells and dendritic cells. They differentiate into T helper 1 (Th1) cells, which produce IFN-γ, activating macrophages and enhancing CTL activity. They also help B cells produce antibodies.
        - **CD8+ T cells (CTLs):** Recognize EBV antigens (e.g., viral proteins) presented on MHC class I molecules by infected B cells. This is the primary mechanism for eliminating infected cells. CTLs release perforin (forms pores in the target cell membrane) and granzymes (enter through the pores and activate caspases, leading to apoptosis).
- **Control of EBV:** The CTL response is critical for controlling the viremia and limiting the spread of EBV-infected B cells. While antibodies are produced, they are less effective at clearing the infection because EBV establishes latency within the B cells, making them less susceptible to antibody-mediated mechanisms like complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) or ADCC, and neutralizing antibodies are less effective against latent infection.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) ADCC involves NK cells or other cytotoxic cells recognizing antibody-coated target cells. While antibodies are produced against EBV, ADCC is not the *most crucial* mechanism for controlling the *intracellular* infection, especially during the acute phase where CTLs are highly active.
    - (B) Complement-mediated lysis requires activation of the complement cascade, often via antibodies (classical pathway) or directly by microbial surfaces (alternative pathway). EBV-infected B cells are intracellular, and complement lysis is not the primary mechanism for controlling this infection.
    - (C) CTLs kill EBV-infected B cells via perforin/granzyme release, inducing apoptosis. Macrophages then phagocytose these apoptotic cells, clearing cellular debris and viral particles. This is a central part of the cell-mediated immune response to intracellular viruses like EBV.
    - (D) Neutralizing antibodies prevent viral entry into cells. While produced, they are less effective against EBV because the virus infects B cells via CD21 and establishes latency intracellularly. CTLs are more effective at eliminating already infected cells.
    - (E) CD4+ T helper cells are important for coordinating the immune response (e.g., activating CTLs, helping B cells), but the direct killing of infected cells is primarily mediated by CD8+ CTLs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) is mediated by NK cells, eosinophils, or macrophages recognizing Fc receptors binding to antibodies coating target cells. While antibodies are produced against EBV, ADCC is not the primary mechanism for controlling the intracellular viral replication within B cells. The main target of CTLs are infected cells presenting viral antigens on MHC class I. NK cells play a role, but CTLs are considered more crucial for controlling EBV viremia.
- (B): Complement-mediated lysis (CDC) is effective against extracellular pathogens or cells coated with antibodies. EBV resides intracellularly within B cells, making it largely inaccessible to complement-mediated lysis. Furthermore, EBV has mechanisms to evade complement.
- (D): Neutralizing antibodies prevent viral entry into susceptible cells. While antibodies are produced against EBV, they are less effective because EBV infects B cells via CD21 (a complement receptor) and establishes latency intracellularly. CTLs are essential for eliminating the already infected cells.
- (E): CD4+ T helper cells are crucial for orchestrating the immune response, including activating CTLs (via IL-2) and B cells (via cytokines and co-stimulation). However, the direct killing of EBV-infected B cells, which is the primary means of controlling viral spread, is performed by CD8+ CTLs. Therefore, while CD4+ T cells are important, CTL activity is the *most crucial* mechanism for eliminating infected cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | EBV Infection Control Mechanism | ADCC (Option A)                     | Complement Lysis (Option B)        | Neutralizing Ab (Option D)         | CD4+ T cell Help (Option E)        |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Primary Cell Type** | CD8+ CTLs                       | NK cells, Macrophages, Eosinophils   | Complement proteins               | Plasma cells (B cells)             | CD4+ T cells                      |
| **Target**           | EBV-infected B cells            | Antibody-coated target cells         | Pathogen surface or Ab-coated cells | Extracellular virus particles      | B cells, CTLs, Macrophages        |
| **Mechanism**        | Perforin/Granzyme, Apoptosis     | Fc receptor binding, Cytotoxicity    | Membrane Attack Complex (MAC)      | Preventing cell entry              | Cytokine secretion, Co-stimulation |
| **Relevance to EBV** | **MOST Crucial**                 | Minor role in controlling viremia    | Minimal role                      | Limited efficacy against latency   | Important, but not *most* crucial |
| **MHC Restriction**  | MHC Class I                     | Not MHC restricted                  | Not MHC restricted                | Not MHC restricted                | MHC Class II (for B cell help)    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cell-mediated immunity, particularly CD8+ CTLs recognizing viral antigens presented on MHC class I, is the primary mechanism for controlling intracellular viral infections like EBV. This contrasts with humoral immunity (antibodies) which is more effective against extracellular pathogens. Remember that EBV infects B cells intracellularly, making CTL-mediated killing the most critical defense. Differentiate this from infections where antibodies (e.g., neutralizing antibodies against extracellular viruses like influenza) or ADCC (e.g., against antibody-coated infected cells) are more prominent.

<a id='question-077'></a>

### Question 077: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 27 (Immunity)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) in recognizing and eliminating intracellular pathogens, specifically viruses, and the mechanism of CTL-mediated killing involving perforin/granzyme release and Fas/FasL interaction. (理解CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞(CTLs)在识别和清除细胞内病原体，特别是病毒中的作用，以及CTL介导的杀伤机制，包括穿孔素/粒酶释放和Fas/FasL相互作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male software engineer presents to his primary care physician with a 3-week history of worsening fatigue, low-grade fever (37.8°C), and a persistent, non-productive cough. He reports no recent travel, sick contacts, or known immunocompromising conditions. He denies smoking or significant occupational exposures. Physical examination reveals mild bilateral cervical lymphadenopathy and scattered rhonchi on lung auscultation. A chest X-ray shows diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis reveals a significantly elevated number of CD8+ T lymphocytes (75% of total cells) and a markedly decreased number of CD4+ T lymphocytes (5% of total cells). Cytology of the BAL fluid shows numerous atypical lymphocytes with large, granular cytoplasm and prominent nucleoli, some exhibiting multinucleation. Viral PCR testing of the BAL fluid is positive for Epstein-Barr virus (EBV). The patient's serum EBV-specific antibody titers (IgM and IgG) are elevated. Given the clinical presentation and laboratory findings, which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the clearance of EBV-infected B lymphocytes in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing Fc receptors on infected B cells.
(B) Complement-mediated lysis of EBV-infected B cells via the classical pathway activation by EBV surface antigens.
(C) Phagocytosis of apoptotic bodies containing EBV-infected B cells by macrophages following Fas/FasL interaction.
(D) Opsonization of EBV-infected B cells by C3b, leading to their uptake by neutrophils.
(E) Direct killing of EBV-infected B cells by CD4+ helper T cells through the release of perforin and granzymes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, suggesting a possible primary EBV infection (infectious mononucleosis) in an immunocompetent adult.
- **Symptoms:** Fatigue, fever, cough, lymphadenopathy, rhonchi, interstitial infiltrates – consistent with systemic viral infection, potentially involving the lungs (atypical presentation of mono).
- **BAL Findings:** High CD8+ T cells, low CD4+ T cells, atypical lymphocytes (likely activated CTLs and reactive lymphocytes), multinucleated cells (syncytia formation, common in EBV).
- **Diagnostic Tests:** Positive EBV PCR and elevated EBV-specific antibodies confirm active EBV infection.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for clearing EBV-infected B lymphocytes. EBV primarily infects B lymphocytes. The immune response involves both humoral (antibodies) and cell-mediated immunity (CTLs). The high CD8+ T cell count in BAL strongly suggests a prominent CTL response.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **EBV Pathogenesis:** EBV infects B lymphocytes, establishing latency. Latently infected B cells express viral proteins (e.g., LMP1, LMP2A, EBNA1) on their surface, which are recognized as foreign by the host immune system.
- **Immune Response to EBV:**
    - **Humoral:** B cells produce antibodies against EBV antigens (IgM early, IgG later). These antibodies can neutralize the virus and mediate ADCC.
    - **Cell-Mediated:** CD8+ CTLs are crucial for controlling EBV infection. CTLs recognize viral peptides presented on MHC class I molecules on the surface of infected B cells.
- **CTL-Mediated Killing Mechanisms:**
    - **Perforin/Granzyme Pathway:** CTLs release perforin (forms pores in the target cell membrane) and granzymes (serine proteases that enter through the pores and activate caspases, leading to apoptosis).
    - **Fas/FasL Pathway:** CTLs express Fas ligand (FasL) on their surface, which binds to Fas (CD95) receptors on the target cell. This interaction triggers the extrinsic apoptotic pathway in the target cell.
- **Role of CD4+ T cells:** CD4+ helper T cells are also involved, primarily by helping B cells produce antibodies and activating macrophages. They do not directly kill infected B cells via perforin/granzyme.
- **NK Cells:** NK cells can kill infected cells via ADCC (recognizing Fc receptors on infected cells coated with antibodies) or through other mechanisms, but CTLs are the primary mediators of specific killing of EBV-infected B cells.
- **Complement:** Complement can be activated, but it's not the primary mechanism for eliminating EBV-infected B cells, especially those expressing viral proteins that might inhibit complement activation or promote cell survival.
- **Phagocytosis:** Apoptotic cells (including infected B cells killed by CTLs) are efficiently cleared by phagocytes (macrophages). The Fas/FasL pathway leads to apoptosis, which is then cleared by phagocytosis.

##### 3. Analysis of Options in Context of EBV and CTLs
- **(A) ADCC by NK cells:** While ADCC occurs in EBV infection, it's mediated by NK cells (or other Fc receptor-bearing cells) recognizing antibodies bound to infected cells. The primary mechanism for specific killing of EBV-infected B cells is CTL-mediated.
- **(B) Complement-mediated lysis:** Complement activation is possible but not the main mechanism for clearing EBV-infected B cells, which often express proteins that inhibit complement.
- **(C) Phagocytosis of apoptotic bodies following Fas/FasL:** CTLs kill target cells (EBV-infected B cells) primarily via perforin/granzyme or Fas/FasL pathways, both leading to apoptosis. Apoptotic cells are then cleared by phagocytosis, mainly by macrophages. This option correctly identifies a key downstream event following CTL killing.
- **(D) Opsonization by C3b and neutrophil uptake:** Similar to (B), complement-mediated opsonization is not the primary mechanism for clearing EBV-infected B cells. Neutrophils are less involved in clearing virally infected cells compared to macrophages clearing apoptotic bodies.
- **(E) Direct killing by CD4+ T cells:** CD4+ T cells are helper cells and do not directly kill target cells via perforin/granzymes. CD8+ T cells (CTLs) are the primary cytotoxic effector cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing Fc receptors on infected B cells.** This describes a valid immune mechanism against virally infected cells or tumor cells, often involving NK cells. However, while ADCC contributes to EBV control, the *most* critical mechanism for controlling EBV-infected B cells, especially given the high CD8+ T cell count in the BAL, is direct CTL killing. CTLs provide specific recognition of viral antigens presented on MHC I, which ADCC lacks.
- **(B) Complement-mediated lysis of EBV-infected B cells via the classical pathway activation by EBV surface antigens.** Complement activation can occur, but EBV-infected B cells often express proteins (like LMP1) that inhibit complement activation or promote cell survival, making complement-mediated lysis less effective as the primary clearance mechanism. Furthermore, CTLs are considered the main effectors.
- **(D) Opsonization of EBV-infected B cells by C3b, leading to their uptake by neutrophils.** This mechanism relies on complement activation and neutrophil phagocytosis. As mentioned for (B), complement is not the primary pathway, and neutrophils are not the main phagocytes involved in clearing apoptotic cells resulting from CTL killing. Macrophages are the primary phagocytes.
- **(E) Direct killing of EBV-infected B cells by CD4+ helper T cells through the release of perforin and granzymes.** CD4+ T cells are helper cells. While they can release cytokines to activate macrophages or help B cells, they do not typically directly kill target cells via perforin/granzymes. This function is characteristic of CD8+ CTLs. The vignette highlights a high CD8+ count, supporting CTL involvement.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | EBV-Infected B Cell Clearance (Correct) | ADCC (Distractor A)                     | Complement Lysis (Distractor B)        | Phagocytosis by Neutrophils (Distractor D) | CD4+ T Cell Killing (Distractor E) |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- |
| **Primary Effector Cell** | CD8+ CTLs                               | NK cells, Macrophages, Neutrophils       | Complement components                  | Neutrophils                                | CD4+ T cells                         |
| **Recognition Mechanism** | MHC Class I + Viral Peptide              | Fc Receptors on Ab-coated target cells | Viral/Cell surface antigens             | C3b receptors                             | TCR (indirectly via cytokines)       |
| **Killing Mechanism**    | Perforin/Granzyme, Fas/FasL -> Apoptosis | Perforin/Granzyme, ADCC receptors -> Apoptosis/Cytotoxicity | Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) -> Lysis | Phagocytosis                               | Perforin/Granzyme (rarely)           |
| **Outcome**              | Apoptosis -> Phagocytosis                | Apoptosis/Cytotoxicity -> Phagocytosis | Lysis                                  | Phagocytosis                                | Apoptosis (uncommon)                 |
| **Relevance to EBV**     | Crucial for controlling latency          | Contributes, but less specific          | Limited role, often inhibited by EBV   | Minor role                                 | Not a primary mechanism               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary mechanism for controlling intracellular viral infections like EBV is cell-mediated immunity, specifically involving CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). CTLs recognize viral antigens presented on MHC class I molecules and kill infected cells via perforin/granzyme release or Fas/FasL interaction, leading to apoptosis and subsequent clearance by phagocytes. Differentiate this from antibody-mediated mechanisms (ADCC, complement, opsonization) which are less specific or less effective against certain intracellular pathogens or cells expressing inhibitory molecules. Remember that CD4+ T cells are primarily helper cells, not direct killers in this context.

<a id='question-078'></a>

### Question 078: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 28 (Figure)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) in recognizing and eliminating intracellular pathogens, specifically viruses, via MHC class I presentation and the subsequent release of cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes, leading to target cell apoptosis. (理解CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞(CTLs)在识别和清除细胞内病原体，特别是病毒的作用，通过MHC I类分子呈递，随后释放含有穿孔素和颗粒酶的细胞毒性颗粒，导致靶细胞凋亡。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male software engineer presents to the clinic with a 3-week history of worsening fatigue, myalgia, and low-grade fever (37.8°C). He reports no recent travel, sick contacts, or specific exposures, but mentions increased stress at work. Physical examination reveals generalized lymphadenopathy and mild hepatosplenomegaly. Initial laboratory tests show elevated liver enzymes (AST 3x ULN, ALT 4x ULN) and mild thrombocytopenia. A monospot test is negative. Further investigation reveals a significantly elevated level of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) in his serum. A liver biopsy shows diffuse hepatocellular necrosis with scattered apoptotic bodies and lymphocytic infiltration, predominantly composed of CD8+ T cells surrounding the affected hepatocytes. Immunohistochemical staining reveals MHC class I upregulation on the surface of the hepatocytes. Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed hepatocellular damage in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing hepatocytes coated with IgG.
(B) Complement-mediated lysis of hepatocytes due to activation of the classical pathway by viral antigens.
(C) Perforin and granzyme release by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes recognizing viral antigens presented on MHC class I molecules.
(D) Fas ligand (FasL) and Fas interaction between activated CD4+ helper T cells and hepatocytes.
(E) Direct cytopathic effect of the virus causing hepatocellular swelling and lysis without significant immune involvement.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Young adult male with fatigue, myalgia, fever, lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, elevated liver enzymes, and thrombocytopenia. This constellation suggests a systemic viral infection affecting the liver.
- **Negative Monospot:** Rules out infectious mononucleosis (EBV), a common cause of similar symptoms.
- **Elevated IFN-γ:** Interferon-gamma is a key cytokine produced primarily by NK cells and T cells (especially Th1 and CTLs) in response to intracellular pathogens, particularly viruses. Its elevation strongly points towards a cell-mediated immune response against an intracellular infection.
- **Liver Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse hepatocellular necrosis, apoptotic bodies, lymphocytic infiltration (predominantly CD8+ T cells), and MHC class I upregulation on hepatocytes. This pattern is characteristic of viral hepatitis where infected hepatocytes are targeted by CTLs. Apoptotic bodies are a hallmark of CTL-mediated killing. MHC class I upregulation is a common strategy by virally infected cells to enhance recognition by CTLs.
- **CD8+ T cell predominance:** Indicates a strong cytotoxic T lymphocyte response, typical for clearing intracellular pathogens like viruses.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for hepatocellular damage, linking the clinical and pathological findings to a specific immune mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Cell-Mediated Immunity (CMI):** The primary defense against intracellular pathogens (viruses, intracellular bacteria).
- **MHC Class I Presentation:** All nucleated cells express MHC class I molecules. Intracellular pathogens (or their antigens) are processed in the cytoplasm and presented on MHC class I molecules on the cell surface.
- **CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs):** Recognize pathogen-derived peptides presented on MHC class I molecules via their T cell receptor (TCR).
- **CTL Activation and Killing:** Upon recognition, CTLs become activated and release cytotoxic granules containing:
    - **Perforin:** Forms pores in the target cell membrane.
    - **Granzymes (e.g., Granzyme B):** Serine proteases that enter the target cell through the perforin pores and activate caspases, initiating the apoptotic cascade.
- **Apoptosis:** Programmed cell death, characterized by cell shrinkage, chromatin condensation, membrane blebbing, and formation of apoptotic bodies, which are phagocytosed by macrophages without causing inflammation. This is the preferred mechanism for eliminating infected cells by CTLs.
- **IFN-γ Role:** Produced by CTLs and Th1 cells, IFN-γ activates macrophages, enhances MHC expression, and promotes differentiation towards Th1 responses, further amplifying CMI.
- **MHC Class I Upregulation:** Virally infected cells often upregulate MHC class I expression to increase the chances of being recognized and killed by CTLs.
- **In this case:** The patient likely has an acute viral hepatitis (though the specific virus isn't identified). The liver biopsy shows CD8+ T cells infiltrating the liver parenchyma, targeting hepatocytes expressing viral antigens on MHC class I. The resulting hepatocellular damage is mediated by CTLs releasing perforin and granzymes, inducing apoptosis in the infected hepatocytes. The elevated IFN-γ supports this strong CMI response.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing hepatocytes coated with IgG.** ADCC involves antibodies (IgG) coating target cells, which are then recognized by Fc receptors on effector cells like NK cells or macrophages. While NK cells can be involved in viral responses, the primary mechanism for eliminating virus-infected hepatocytes is CTL-mediated killing via MHC class I presentation, not ADCC. The liver biopsy shows CD8+ T cells, not predominantly NK cells mediating ADCC. ADCC typically involves IgG coating, which isn't the primary mechanism described here.
- (B) **Complement-mediated lysis of hepatocytes due to activation of the classical pathway by viral antigens.** The classical complement pathway is activated by antigen-antibody complexes. While complement can play a role in some viral infections, it's not the primary mechanism for eliminating virus-infected hepatocytes. CTL-mediated apoptosis is the dominant pathway. Furthermore, complement-mediated lysis typically causes inflammation, whereas CTL-mediated apoptosis is generally non-inflammatory.
- (D) **Fas ligand (FasL) and Fas interaction between activated CD4+ helper T cells and hepatocytes.** FasL/Fas interaction is another pathway for inducing apoptosis. While CD4+ T cells can express FasL, the primary cytotoxic mechanism against virus-infected cells presenting antigen on MHC class I is mediated by CD8+ CTLs via perforin/granzymes. CD4+ T cells primarily help activate CTLs and macrophages. The biopsy shows CD8+ T cells as the predominant infiltrating lymphocytes targeting the hepatocytes.
- (E) **Direct cytopathic effect of the virus causing hepatocellular swelling and lysis without significant immune involvement.** While viruses can directly damage cells, the prominent lymphocytic infiltrate (especially CD8+ T cells), MHC class I upregulation, and apoptotic bodies strongly indicate a significant immune response is actively eliminating infected cells. The immune response, specifically CMI, is the major driver of the observed hepatocellular damage in this context.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - CTL Killing (Perforin/Granzymes) | Option A (ADCC)                                  | Option B (Complement)                            | Option D (FasL/Fas by CD4+)                      | Option E (Direct Viral Cytopathy)                 |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- |
| **Effector Cell**        | CD8+ CTL                                             | NK cells, Macrophages                             | Complement components                             | CD4+ T cells (less common for this scenario)     | Virus itself                                      |
| **Target Recognition**   | Viral Ag on MHC Class I                             | Antibody (IgG) on target cell                     | Antibody (IgG/IgM) on target cell                 | Fas on target cell                               | Viral proteins/replication machinery              |
| **Mechanism**            | Perforin pores + Granzyme-induced apoptosis          | Fc receptor binding + Cytotoxicity                 | Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) formation + lysis | FasL binding to Fas receptor -> apoptosis        | Viral replication, membrane disruption, etc.      |
| **Inflammation**         | Minimal (Apoptosis)                                  | Variable                                          | Significant (Inflammation)                        | Minimal (Apoptosis)                               | Variable                                          |
| **Key Immune Markers**   | CD8+ T cells, IFN-γ, MHC Class I upregulation        | IgG, NK cells, Fc receptors                       | C3a, C5a, C5b-9                                  | FasL, Fas                                        | Viral antigens                                    |
| **Clinical Scenario Fit** | Viral hepatitis with CD8+ infiltrate, apoptosis     | Less common for direct hepatocyte killing        | Not primary mechanism for viral hepatitis        | Not primary mechanism for viral hepatitis        | Immune response is significant, not direct effect |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary mechanism for eliminating virus-infected cells is cell-mediated immunity, specifically involving CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing viral antigens presented on MHC class I molecules. CTLs induce apoptosis in target cells via the release of perforin and granzymes. Key differentials include ADCC (mediated by NK cells/macrophages via antibodies), complement-mediated lysis, FasL/Fas interaction, and direct viral cytopathic effects, but CTL-mediated killing is the hallmark of CMI against intracellular pathogens like viruses.

<a id='question-079'></a>

### Question 079: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 29 (Mediated)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) in recognizing and eliminating intracellular pathogens, specifically viruses, and the mechanism by which they induce apoptosis in infected target cells. (理解CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞(CTLs)在识别和清除细胞内病原体，特别是病毒中的作用，以及它们如何诱导感染靶细胞凋亡的机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male software engineer presents to his primary care physician with a 3-week history of worsening fatigue, myalgia, and low-grade fever (37.8°C). He reports no recent travel, sick contacts, or specific exposures, although he admits to working long hours in a shared office space. His physical examination is largely unremarkable except for mild cervical lymphadenopathy. Initial laboratory tests show elevated liver enzymes (AST 3x ULN, ALT 4x ULN) and mild thrombocytopenia (platelets 120,000/µL). A viral panel is ordered, and testing reveals acute infection with Epstein-Barr virus (EBV). The patient is diagnosed with infectious mononucleosis. During the course of his illness, the patient's immune system mounts a response against EBV-infected B lymphocytes. Which of the following mechanisms is primarily responsible for the elimination of EBV-infected B cells by the adaptive immune system?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing Fc receptors on infected B cells.
(B) Complement-mediated lysis of infected B cells following opsonization by EBV-specific antibodies.
(C) Perforin and granzyme release by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing viral antigens presented on MHC class I molecules.
(D) Phagocytosis of infected B cells by macrophages after opsonization with C3b.
(E) Activation-induced cell death (AICD) of infected B cells mediated by Fas ligand (FasL) on helper T cells binding to Fas receptors on B cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with fatigue, myalgia, fever, lymphadenopathy, elevated liver enzymes, and thrombocytopenia. This constellation of symptoms is classic for infectious mononucleosis.
- **Diagnosis:** Acute EBV infection confirmed by viral panel. EBV primarily infects B lymphocytes.
- **Pathophysiology:** EBV is a DNA virus that establishes latent infection in B cells. The immune response, particularly the cell-mediated response, is crucial for controlling the infection.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the primary mechanism by which the adaptive immune system eliminates EBV-infected B cells. This points towards a cell-mediated cytotoxic process.
- **Key Clue:** EBV infects B cells *intracellularly*. Intracellular pathogens, especially viruses, are typically eliminated by cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **EBV Infection:** EBV infects B cells, causing them to proliferate. This proliferation is a hallmark of infectious mononucleosis.
- **Immune Response to EBV:** The adaptive immune response involves both humoral (antibody) and cell-mediated immunity.
    - **Humoral:** Antibodies are produced against EBV antigens, but they are not the primary mechanism for eliminating infected B cells. Antibodies can neutralize free virus and mediate ADCC, but the main target is the infected cell itself.
    - **Cell-Mediated:** CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) are critical. EBV-infected B cells process viral antigens and present them on MHC class I molecules on their surface. CTLs recognize these MHC class I-peptide complexes via their T cell receptor (TCR). Upon recognition, CTLs release cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes. Perforin forms pores in the target cell membrane, allowing granzymes (serine proteases) to enter the cell and activate caspases, leading to apoptosis (programmed cell death). This is the primary mechanism for clearing virus-infected cells.
- **Option (C) Mechanism:** CD8+ CTLs recognize viral peptides presented on MHC class I molecules on the surface of infected B cells. This recognition triggers the release of perforin and granzymes, which induce apoptosis in the infected B cell. This is the canonical mechanism for eliminating intracellular pathogens like viruses.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC):** NK cells (and other cells like macrophages, neutrophils) express Fc receptors (FcγRIIIa or CD16) that bind to the Fc portion of antibodies coating target cells. While antibodies are produced against EBV, and ADCC can contribute to eliminating infected cells, it is not the *primary* mechanism for clearing intracellular viral infections. CTLs are the main players in this scenario. NK cells are more important for eliminating cells lacking MHC class I expression or cells infected with viruses that downregulate MHC class I.
- **(B) Complement-mediated lysis:** Antibodies can activate the classical complement pathway, leading to the deposition of C3b (opsonization) and the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC). While complement can play a role, it is generally less effective against intact cells and is not the primary mechanism for eliminating virus-infected B cells, especially compared to CTL-mediated killing. Furthermore, viruses often have mechanisms to evade complement.
- **(D) Phagocytosis by macrophages:** Macrophages can phagocytose opsonized cells (e.g., coated with C3b or antibodies). While macrophages are involved in clearing cellular debris and apoptotic bodies resulting from CTL killing, they are not the primary effector cells directly killing the infected B cells via opsonization in this context.
- **(E) Activation-induced cell death (AICD):** AICD is a form of apoptosis triggered by excessive T cell receptor (TCR) stimulation. It involves the interaction of Fas ligand (FasL) on activated T cells (often helper T cells, but potentially CTLs) with Fas receptors (CD95) on target cells (like B cells). While AICD can contribute to regulating the immune response and eliminating chronically activated or autoreactive lymphocytes, it is not the primary mechanism by which CTLs eliminate acutely infected B cells presenting viral antigens on MHC class I. The primary CTL mechanism is perforin/granzyme-mediated cytotoxicity.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - CTL Killing | Distractor (A) - ADCC (NK cells) | Distractor (B) - Complement Lysis | Distractor (D) - Macrophage Phagocytosis | Distractor (E) - AICD |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------------------------------ | :------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :------------------- |
| **Effector Cell**       | CD8+ CTL                           | NK cell                         | Complement components            | Macrophage                               | Activated T cell     |
| **Target Recognition**  | MHC Class I + Viral Peptide       | Antibody (Fc) on target cell    | Antibody or C-reactive protein   | Opsonins (Ab, C3b) on target cell        | FasL on T cell       |
| **Target Cell Type**    | Virus-infected cells               | Antibody-coated cells           | Cells with activated complement  | Opsonized cells                          | Activated lymphocytes|
| **Mechanism of Killing** | Perforin/Granzyme -> Apoptosis     | Perforin/Granzyme -> Apoptosis  | MAC formation -> Cell lysis       | Phagocytosis                              | FasL/Fas -> Apoptosis |
| **Primary Role in EBV** | Eliminating infected B cells      | Minor role                      | Minor role                       | Clearing debris                           | Immune regulation    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary mechanism for eliminating virus-infected cells (like EBV-infected B cells in infectious mononucleosis) is cell-mediated cytotoxicity by CD8+ CTLs recognizing viral antigens presented on MHC class I molecules, leading to apoptosis via perforin and granzymes. Differentiate this from antibody-mediated mechanisms (ADCC, complement, opsonization/phagocytosis) which are more relevant for extracellular pathogens or antibody-coated cells, and AICD which is primarily involved in immune regulation.

<a id='question-080'></a>

### Question 080: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 30 (Immunity)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) in eliminating intracellular pathogens, specifically viruses, and the mechanism by which they recognize and kill infected cells via MHC class I presentation and perforin/granzyme pathways. (理解CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞(CTLs)在清除细胞内病原体，特别是病毒中的作用，以及它们通过MHC I类呈递和穿孔素/颗粒酶途径识别和杀死受感染细胞的机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male software engineer presents to the clinic with a 3-week history of worsening fatigue, low-grade fever (37.8°C), and a persistent, non-productive cough. He reports no recent travel, sick contacts, or known immunocompromising conditions. He denies smoking or significant occupational exposures. Physical examination reveals mild bilateral cervical lymphadenopathy and scattered rhonchi on lung auscultation. Chest X-ray shows diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis reveals a significantly elevated number of CD8+ T lymphocytes (75% of total cells) and a markedly reduced number of CD4+ T lymphocytes (5% of total cells). Cytology of the BAL fluid shows numerous cells with large, eosinophilic intranuclear inclusions, characteristic of viral cytopathic effects. Viral culture of the BAL fluid is positive for Cytomegalovirus (CMV). The patient's serum CMV IgM and IgG levels are elevated, indicating a recent primary infection. Given the clinical presentation and laboratory findings, which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for controlling the CMV infection in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing CMV-infected cells coated with CMV-specific IgG.
(B) Complement-mediated lysis of CMV-infected cells following opsonization by CMV-specific antibodies.
(C) Phagocytosis of CMV virions and infected cells by alveolar macrophages expressing Fc receptors.
(D) Direct killing of CMV-infected cells by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing viral antigens presented on MHC class I molecules.
(E) Activation of CD4+ T helper cells leading to the production of IFN-γ, which inhibits viral replication within infected cells.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, immunocompetent (no stated history of immunosuppression).
- **Symptoms:** Fatigue, low-grade fever, non-productive cough (suggestive of viral respiratory infection).
- **Physical Exam:** Cervical lymphadenopathy, rhonchi (consistent with respiratory infection).
- **Imaging:** Diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates on chest X-ray (common in viral pneumonitis).
- **BAL Fluid Analysis:**
    - Significantly elevated CD8+ T cells (75%): Indicates a strong cell-mediated immune response, particularly cytotoxic T cell activity.
    - Markedly reduced CD4+ T cells (5%): While low, this doesn't necessarily indicate immunodeficiency in the context of a strong viral infection where CTLs are recruited.
    - Cytology: Large, eosinophilic intranuclear inclusions (characteristic of CMV cytopathic effect).
- **Virology:** BAL fluid positive for CMV; elevated CMV IgM and IgG (confirms recent primary CMV infection).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism controlling the CMV infection.

The key clues are the confirmation of CMV infection (viral culture, serology, inclusions) and the prominent increase in CD8+ T cells in the BAL fluid from the lungs, the primary site of infection. CMV is a DNA virus that establishes latency within host cells. Control of such intracellular viral infections relies heavily on cell-mediated immunity, specifically CTLs.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **CMV Pathogenesis:** CMV infects various cell types, including epithelial cells, endothelial cells, and immune cells. It replicates within the nucleus, causing characteristic cytopathic effects (intranuclear inclusions).
- **Immune Response to CMV:**
    - **Innate Immunity:** Interferons (IFN-α/β) produced by infected cells limit viral replication. NK cells can kill infected cells, especially those with reduced MHC class I expression (a viral evasion strategy).
    - **Adaptive Immunity:**
        - **Humoral (Antibody):** Antibodies (IgG, IgM) are produced, but they are less effective at clearing established intracellular viral infections. They can neutralize extracellular virions and mediate ADCC.
        - **Cell-Mediated (T cell):** This is crucial for controlling CMV.
            - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize viral antigens presented on MHC class II molecules by antigen-presenting cells (APCs). They differentiate into T helper 1 (Th1) cells, producing IFN-γ, which activates macrophages to kill intracellular viruses and enhances MHC expression. They also help activate CTLs.
            - **CD8+ T cells (CTLs):** Recognize viral antigens (peptides derived from CMV proteins) presented on MHC class I molecules on the surface of *any* infected host cell. This is the primary mechanism for eliminating virus-infected cells. CTLs kill target cells via:
                - **Perforin/Granzyme Pathway:** CTLs release perforin (forms pores in the target cell membrane) and granzymes (serine proteases that enter through the pores and induce apoptosis).
                - **Fas/FasL Pathway:** CTLs express Fas ligand (FasL), which binds to Fas receptor on target cells, triggering apoptosis.
- **Focus on the Vignette:** The high percentage of CD8+ T cells in the BAL fluid strongly points to CTL activity as the dominant mechanism controlling the infection in the lungs. CTLs recognize CMV antigens presented on MHC class I of infected lung epithelial cells and other local cells, leading to their killing.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing CMV-infected cells coated with CMV-specific IgG.** While ADCC can occur, it's primarily mediated by NK cells, macrophages, and neutrophils, not just NK cells. More importantly, CTLs are the *primary* mechanism for controlling intracellular viral infections like CMV, especially given the high number of CD8+ T cells found in the BAL. ADCC is less efficient for clearing cells already infected intracellularly.
- (B) **Complement-mediated lysis of CMV-infected cells following opsonization by CMV-specific antibodies.** Complement activation and lysis are generally ineffective against intracellular pathogens residing within host cells. Antibodies primarily target extracellular pathogens or virions. While complement can play a role in clearing extracellular virions, it's not the main mechanism for controlling CMV infection within host cells.
- (C) **Phagocytosis of CMV virions and infected cells by alveolar macrophages expressing Fc receptors.** Macrophages can phagocytose virions and infected cells (opsonized by antibodies or complement), but this is not the primary mechanism for controlling CMV, which establishes intracellular infection and latency. CTLs are more effective at eliminating infected cells.
- (E) **Activation of CD4+ T helper cells leading to the production of IFN-γ, which inhibits viral replication within infected cells.** CD4+ T cells and IFN-γ are crucial for *supporting* the CTL response and activating macrophages. However, the direct killing of infected cells is primarily carried out by CD8+ CTLs recognizing MHC class I. While IFN-γ helps, the question asks for the mechanism *controlling* the infection, which is the direct killing by CTLs, strongly suggested by the high CD8+ T cell count in the affected tissue. The vignette highlights the *effector* cells (CD8+ T cells) rather than the helper cells (CD4+ T cells).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CMV Infection Control (Correct Answer D)                                  | ADCC (Option A)                                       | Complement Lysis (Option B)                             | Macrophage Phagocytosis (Option C)                      | CD4+ Th1 Response (Option E)                               |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Immune Cell** | CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs)                                       | NK cells, Macrophages, Neutrophils                     | Complement proteins, Phagocytes (secondary)             | Alveolar Macrophages                                     | CD4+ T Helper 1 cells                                       |
| **Target**              | Virus-infected host cells                                                 | Antibody-coated target cells                           | Antibody/Complement-coated pathogens/cells              | Virions, Infected cells, Debris                           | Infected cells (indirectly via IFN-γ)                       |
| **Mechanism**           | Recognition via MHC Class I, Perforin/Granzyme, Fas/FasL                  | Fc receptor binding, Release of cytotoxic granules     | Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) formation                 | Engulfment, Intracellular killing                         | IFN-γ production, Macrophage activation, CTL help          |
| **Effectiveness vs. Intracellular Pathogens** | Highly effective (primary mechanism)                               | Less effective for established intracellular infection | Ineffective against intracellular pathogens              | Limited effectiveness against established infection       | Important for supporting CTL response, less direct killing |
| **Vignette Support**    | High CD8+ T cells in BAL, Intranuclear inclusions (viral cytopathic effect) | None specific                                         | None specific                                            | None specific                                            | Low CD4+ T cells in BAL (less emphasis)                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cell-mediated immunity, particularly CD8+ CTLs recognizing viral antigens presented on MHC class I, is the primary mechanism for controlling intracellular viral infections like CMV. High numbers of CD8+ T cells at the site of infection strongly indicate CTL-mediated clearance. Differentiate this from antibody-mediated mechanisms (ADCC, complement, phagocytosis) which are less effective against established intracellular pathogens, and from CD4+ T cell help, which is crucial but secondary to the direct killing by CTLs.
