<a id='question-088'></a>

### Question 088: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 38 (signal)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of intracellular signaling pathways, specifically the JAK-STAT pathway, in mediating the effects of cytokines like IFN-γ in cell-mediated immunity, particularly in macrophage activation and antigen presentation. (理解细胞介导免疫中细胞内信号通路，特别是JAK-STAT通路，在细胞因子（如IFN-γ）介导的巨噬细胞激活和抗原呈递中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of fever, night sweats, and a persistent, non-productive cough. He is a former smoker and works as a construction worker, frequently exposed to dust and debris. Physical examination reveals bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy and scattered crackles in the lower lung fields. A chest X-ray shows diffuse interstitial infiltrates. Sputum analysis is negative for acid-fast bacilli (AFB) on initial smear, but culture is pending. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis shows a significantly elevated number of CD68+ macrophages. Further investigation reveals that these macrophages exhibit impaired phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria and reduced expression of MHC class II molecules on their surface. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *JAK2* gene, leading to a loss-of-function protein.

Which of the following intracellular signaling pathways is most likely impaired in this patient's macrophages, explaining their functional defects?

(A) NF-κB pathway
(B) MAPK pathway
(C) PI3K/Akt pathway
(D) JAK-STAT pathway
(E) mTOR pathway

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with fever, night sweats, cough, hilar lymphadenopathy, interstitial infiltrates – suggestive of chronic granulomatous disease (CGD) or an atypical mycobacterial/fungal infection, or even sarcoidosis, but the specific immune defect points elsewhere.
- **Exposure History:** Construction worker (dust exposure) – relevant for pneumoconiosis, silicosis, but less directly related to the specific immune defect. Smoking history is less relevant here.
- **Diagnostic Findings:**
    - Negative AFB smear but pending culture: Rules out typical TB initially, but doesn't exclude atypical mycobacteria or other infections.
    - Elevated CD68+ macrophages in BAL: Indicates macrophage involvement in the lung pathology.
    - Impaired phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria: Suggests a defect in macrophage function, specifically in the effector phase after opsonization.
    - Reduced MHC class II expression: Indicates impaired antigen presentation, a key function of macrophages.
    - Homozygous *JAK2* loss-of-function mutation: This is the crucial genetic clue pointing to a specific signaling pathway defect.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the impaired intracellular signaling pathway responsible for the macrophage dysfunction.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **IFN-γ Signaling:** Interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) is a critical cytokine for activating macrophages. It binds to the IFN-γ receptor (IFNGR), which is a heterodimer consisting of IFNGR1 and IFNGR2 subunits.
- **JAK-STAT Pathway Activation:** Binding of IFN-γ to its receptor triggers receptor dimerization and activation of associated Janus kinases (JAKs), specifically JAK1 and JAK2. Activated JAKs phosphorylate tyrosine residues on the receptor subunits (Signal Transducer and Activator of Transcription, STATs), primarily STAT1.
- **STAT1 Dimerization and Translocation:** Phosphorylated STAT1 molecules dimerize and translocate to the nucleus.
- **Gene Transcription:** In the nucleus, STAT1 dimers bind to specific DNA sequences (GAS elements) in the promoter regions of target genes, such as those encoding MHC class II molecules, components of the phagosome maturation pathway (e.g., LAMPs), and other proteins involved in macrophage activation and antigen presentation.
- **Patient's Defect:** The patient has a homozygous loss-of-function mutation in *JAK2*. This means that even when IFN-γ binds to its receptor, JAK2 cannot be activated, preventing the phosphorylation of STAT1 and subsequent downstream gene transcription.
- **Consequences:** The lack of functional JAK2 leads to impaired IFN-γ signaling, resulting in:
    - Reduced MHC class II expression on macrophages (impaired antigen presentation).
    - Defective phagosome maturation and impaired killing of phagocytosed microbes (impaired phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria).
    - Overall impaired macrophage activation.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (fever, night sweats, cough, lung infiltrates) and macrophage defects are consistent with impaired cell-mediated immunity due to defective IFN-γ signaling. While the presentation might mimic infections like TB or CGD, the specific genetic defect points directly to the JAK-STAT pathway.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **NF-κB pathway:** This pathway is crucial for inflammation and innate immunity, often activated by TLRs and cytokines like TNF-α and IL-1. It regulates the expression of pro-inflammatory cytokines, adhesion molecules, and survival factors. While important for macrophage function, it is not the primary pathway activated by IFN-γ for MHC class II induction and phagosome maturation. Defects in NF-κB signaling are associated with different immunodeficiencies (e.g., NEMO deficiency).
- (B) **MAPK pathway:** Mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) pathways (ERK, JNK, p38) are involved in various cellular processes, including proliferation, differentiation, and stress responses. They can be activated by various stimuli, including cytokines and growth factors, and play roles in inflammation and apoptosis. However, the primary signaling cascade downstream of IFN-γ receptor activation for MHC class II induction and macrophage activation is JAK-STAT, not MAPK.
- (C) **PI3K/Akt pathway:** The phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase (PI3K)/Akt pathway is involved in cell growth, survival, metabolism, and cytoskeletal rearrangement. It is activated by various growth factors and cytokines and plays roles in immune cell development and function. While it can interact with other pathways, it is not the main pathway mediating IFN-γ's effects on MHC class II expression and phagosome maturation.
- (E) **mTOR pathway:** The mammalian target of rapamycin (mTOR) pathway is a central regulator of cell growth, proliferation, survival, and metabolism. It integrates signals from growth factors, nutrients, and energy status. While mTOR signaling can influence immune cell function, it is not the direct downstream effector of IFN-γ receptor signaling for macrophage activation in the way that JAK-STAT is.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | JAK-STAT Pathway (Correct) | NF-κB Pathway | MAPK Pathway | PI3K/Akt Pathway | mTOR Pathway |
|-------------------------|-----------------------------|----------------|--------------|-----------------|--------------|
| **Primary Cytokine Signal** | IFN-γ, IL-6, IL-10, IL-23   | TNF-α, IL-1β  | Various      | Growth Factors | Nutrients    |
| **Key Function in Macrophages** | MHC II expression, Phagosome maturation | Pro-inflammatory cytokine production | Cell differentiation, stress response | Cell growth, survival | Cell growth, metabolism |
| **Receptor Association** | JAKs (e.g., JAK2)           | IKK complex   | MAPKKKs      | PI3K           | mTORC1/2    |
| **Downstream Effectors** | STAT transcription factors | NF-κB transcription factors | MAP kinases   | Akt kinase    | S6K1, 4E-BP1 |
| **Defect Consequence**  | Impaired IFN-γ response, reduced MHC II, defective phagosome maturation | Impaired inflammatory response | Altered cell differentiation/stress response | Impaired cell growth/survival | Impaired cell growth/metabolism |
| **Relevant Disease**     | JAK2 deficiency (as in vignette) | NEMO deficiency | Various      | Various        | Various      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The JAK-STAT pathway is the primary intracellular signaling cascade activated by IFN-γ to induce MHC class II expression and enhance macrophage activation, including phagosome maturation. Defects in this pathway, such as mutations in JAK2, lead to impaired cell-mediated immunity and susceptibility to intracellular pathogens. Differentiating this from defects in other pathways (NF-κB, MAPK, PI3K/Akt, mTOR) requires understanding the specific cytokine signals and downstream functions mediated by each pathway.

<a id='question-089'></a>

### Question 089: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 39 (Activation)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of antigen presentation by MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) to CD4+ T helper cells, leading to T cell activation, cytokine production (specifically IL-2), and subsequent proliferation and differentiation, which is crucial for orchestrating adaptive immune responses, including cell-mediated immunity. (理解MHC II类分子在抗原提呈细胞(APCs)上向CD4+ T辅助细胞呈递抗原的作用，导致T细胞激活、细胞因子产生(特别是IL-2)、后续增殖和分化，这对于协调适应性免疫应答，包括细胞介导的免疫至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of progressively worsening cough, fever, night sweats, and weight loss. He is a previously healthy construction worker who recently completed a renovation project in an old building known to harbor pigeons. Physical examination reveals bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy and scattered crackles in the lower lung fields. A chest X-ray shows diffuse interstitial infiltrates. Sputum analysis reveals acid-fast bacilli. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid is sent for cell counts and analysis. Flow cytometry of BAL fluid shows a significant increase in CD4+ T cells expressing CD69, an early activation marker, and CD25, the IL-2 receptor alpha chain. Furthermore, the BAL fluid contains elevated levels of IL-2. The patient is started on standard anti-tuberculosis therapy.

Which of the following cellular events is most directly responsible for the observed increase in CD4+ T cell activation and IL-2 production in this patient's BAL fluid?

(A) Binding of CD8+ T cells to MHC class I molecules presenting viral antigens on infected hepatocytes.
(B) Recognition of bacterial lipopolysaccharide (LPS) by Toll-like receptor 4 (TLR4) on B cells, leading to antibody production.
(C) Presentation of *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* antigens by MHC class II molecules on alveolar macrophages to CD4+ T cells.
(D) Interaction of CD19+ B cells with complement-opsonized bacterial capsules via Fc receptors.
(E) Activation of NK cells by binding to cells lacking MHC class I expression, leading to the release of perforin and granzymes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male, construction worker, exposure to pigeons (potential source of *Cryptococcus neoformans* or *Histoplasma capsulatum*, but the acid-fast bacilli point strongly towards *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*). Symptoms (cough, fever, night sweats, weight loss) are classic for tuberculosis. Physical exam (hilar lymphadenopathy, crackles) and chest X-ray (interstitial infiltrates) are consistent with pulmonary TB.
- **Diagnostic Clue:** Sputum analysis reveals acid-fast bacilli, confirming infection with *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*.
- **Immunological Clue:** BAL fluid analysis shows increased CD4+ T cells expressing CD69 (early activation marker) and CD25 (IL-2 receptor alpha chain). Elevated IL-2 levels are also present.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the cellular event directly responsible for the observed CD4+ T cell activation and IL-2 production.
- **Connecting Clues:** *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* is an intracellular pathogen primarily handled by cell-mediated immunity. Macrophages phagocytose the bacteria but often cannot kill them. This leads to the presentation of mycobacterial antigens via MHC class II molecules on the surface of infected macrophages (APCs). These MHC II-antigen complexes are recognized by the T cell receptor (TCR) on CD4+ T helper cells. This interaction, along with co-stimulatory signals (e.g., B7 on APC binding to CD28 on T cell), triggers T cell activation. Activated CD4+ T cells proliferate (requiring IL-2, which they also produce in an autocrine loop) and differentiate into effector T helper cells (e.g., Th1 cells in TB), which secrete cytokines like IFN-γ to activate macrophages to kill the intracellular bacteria. The presence of activated CD4+ T cells (CD69+, CD25+) and IL-2 in the BAL fluid directly reflects this process.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Cell-Mediated Immunity Activation:** The core process involves antigen presentation.
    - **Antigen-Presenting Cells (APCs):** Professional APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells) phagocytose or endocytose pathogens or their antigens.
    - **Antigen Processing:** Intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* are degraded within APCs into peptides.
    - **MHC Class II Presentation:** These peptides are loaded onto MHC class II molecules within endosomes/lysosomes. The MHC II-peptide complex is then transported to the cell surface.
    - **T Cell Recognition:** CD4+ T helper cells have TCRs specific for the presented peptide-MHC II complex. Binding of the TCR to the complex, along with co-stimulation (e.g., CD28 on T cell binding to B7 on APC), initiates T cell activation.
    - **T Cell Activation Cascade:** TCR signaling leads to activation of intracellular signaling pathways (e.g., involving Lck, ZAP-70, PLCγ, PKC, calcineurin). This results in the upregulation of transcription factors (e.g., NFAT, AP-1) that drive the expression of genes for IL-2, IL-2 receptor (CD25), CD69, and other activation markers.
    - **IL-2 Production:** IL-2 is a crucial T cell growth factor. It binds to the IL-2 receptor (CD25 is the alpha chain) on the same T cell (autocrine) and other T cells (paracrine), promoting proliferation and differentiation into effector and memory T cells.
- **Specifics for *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*:** Macrophages are the primary APCs presenting *M. tuberculosis* antigens via MHC II to CD4+ T cells. This interaction is critical for controlling the infection. The observed CD4+ T cell activation (CD69+, CD25+) and IL-2 production in the BAL fluid are direct consequences of this antigen presentation and T cell recognition process.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Binding of CD8+ T cells to MHC class I molecules presenting viral antigens on infected hepatocytes. This describes the activation of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs), which are crucial for eliminating virus-infected cells or tumor cells. CTLs recognize antigens presented on MHC class I molecules (found on almost all nucleated cells), not MHC class II. While CTLs play a role in some aspects of TB immunity, the primary driver of the observed CD4+ T cell activation and IL-2 production in the BAL fluid of a patient with pulmonary TB is the interaction between CD4+ T cells and MHC class II-presenting APCs (macrophages) infected with *M. tuberculosis*. This option involves the wrong T cell subset (CD8+ vs. CD4+) and the wrong MHC restriction (MHC I vs. MHC II).
- (B): Recognition of bacterial lipopolysaccharide (LPS) by Toll-like receptor 4 (TLR4) on B cells, leading to antibody production. LPS is a component of Gram-negative bacterial cell walls. TLR4 is expressed primarily on myeloid cells (macrophages, dendritic cells), not B cells. While TLR4 signaling can activate APCs, leading to cytokine production and T cell activation, this option incorrectly identifies B cells as the primary TLR4-expressing cells and focuses on antibody production, which is primarily mediated by B cells and is less central to the initial activation of CD4+ T cells in response to intracellular pathogens like *M. tuberculosis*. The key event here is antigen presentation via MHC II to CD4+ T cells.
- (D): Interaction of CD19+ B cells with complement-opsonized bacterial capsules via Fc receptors. CD19 is a B cell marker. B cells can act as APCs, but their primary role in this context is antibody production. Interaction with complement-opsonized bacteria via Fc receptors is a mechanism for B cell activation and antigen uptake, leading to antibody responses. This process does not directly explain the activation of CD4+ T cells expressing CD69 and CD25 and producing IL-2, which are hallmarks of T cell activation in response to presented antigens via MHC II.
- (E): Activation of NK cells by binding to cells lacking MHC class I expression, leading to the release of perforin and granzymes. Natural Killer (NK) cells are part of the innate immune system. They recognize and kill cells that have lost MHC class I expression (e.g., virus-infected cells or tumor cells) or express stress ligands. NK cell activation does not involve TCR recognition of peptide-MHC complexes or the production of IL-2 by T cells. While NK cells contribute to TB immunity, the specific findings of activated CD4+ T cells (CD69+, CD25+) and IL-2 production point directly to the adaptive T cell response triggered by antigen presentation via MHC II.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C)                                                                                                                               | Option A (CD8+ T cells/MHC I)                                                                                                | Option B (B cells/TLR4/LPS)                                                                                                  | Option D (B cells/FcR)                                                                                                     | Option E (NK cells)                                                                                                         |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Cell Type**   | CD4+ T helper cells                                                                                                                             | CD8+ Cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs)                                                                                             | B cells                                                                                                                       | B cells                                                                                                                       | NK cells                                                                                                                       |
| **MHC Restriction**     | MHC Class II                                                                                                                                   | MHC Class I                                                                                                                    | N/A (Direct recognition of LPS via TLR4, or antigen presentation via MHC II/III)                                                | N/A (Direct recognition via FcR, or antigen presentation via MHC II/III)                                                       | N/A (Recognition via inhibitory/activating receptors, not MHC-dependent TCR)                                                     |
| **Antigen Source**      | Intracellular pathogens (like *M. tuberculosis*) presented by APCs (macrophages)                                                               | Viral antigens presented by infected cells                                                                                        | Bacterial antigens (e.g., LPS), or antigens presented via MHC II/III                                                           | Bacterial antigens (e.g., capsules)                                                                                           | Cells lacking MHC I or expressing stress ligands                                                                                |
| **Key Outcome**         | T cell activation (CD69+, CD25+), IL-2 production, proliferation, differentiation into Th1 cells (in TB), help for B cells and CTLs             | Cytotoxicity (perforin/granzymes), killing of infected/tumor cells                                                              | Antibody production, antigen presentation                                                                                       | Antibody production, antigen presentation                                                                                       | Cytotoxicity (perforin/granzymes), killing of target cells                                                                     |
| **Relevance to TB**     | Central role in orchestrating cell-mediated immunity against intracellular *M. tuberculosis* via macrophage activation (IFN-γ production). | CTLs contribute, but CD4+ T cells are crucial for macrophage activation and controlling primary infection.                     | B cells and antibodies play a limited role in controlling active TB infection.                                                  | B cells and antibodies play a limited role in controlling active TB infection.                                                  | NK cells contribute to early control and granuloma formation, but CD4+ T cell activation is the key event described in the vignette. |
| **Activation Markers**  | CD69 (early), CD25 (IL-2Rα)                                                                                                                    | CD69 (early), CD25 (IL-2Rα)                                                                                                    | CD69 (early), CD25 (IL-2Rα) upon activation                                                                                    | CD69 (early), CD25 (IL-2Rα) upon activation                                                                                    | N/A (Different activation markers)                                                                                             |
| **Cytokine Production** | IL-2 (autocrine growth factor), IFN-γ (macrophage activation), TNF-α                                                                            | IFN-γ, TNF-α, Granzyme B, Perforin                                                                                             | IL-4, IL-5, IL-6, IL-10, IL-13 (depending on differentiation), Cytokines for T cell help                                     | IL-4, IL-5, IL-6, IL-10, IL-13 (depending on differentiation), Cytokines for T cell help                                     | IFN-γ, TNF-α                                                                                                                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The activation of CD4+ T helper cells by APCs presenting antigens via MHC class II is a fundamental step in cell-mediated immunity, particularly against intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*. This process involves TCR recognition, co-stimulation, and subsequent T cell proliferation and cytokine production (e.g., IL-2), leading to effector functions. Differentiating this process from CD8+ T cell activation (MHC I), B cell activation (TLR4/FcR), and NK cell activation (MHC I downregulation) is crucial. Remember that CD4+ T cells are the primary orchestrators of the adaptive immune response against intracellular bacteria by activating macrophages.

<a id='question-090'></a>

### Question 090: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 40 (provides)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on activated T cells in providing co-stimulation to B cells for isotype switching and affinity maturation, and the consequences of its deficiency. (理解CD40L在激活的T细胞中提供给B细胞的共刺激作用，以进行同种异型转换和亲和力成熟，以及其缺乏的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections starting at 6 months of age. He has had multiple episodes of pneumonia, otitis media, and sinusitis, often requiring hospitalization. He has not received any live vaccines. Physical examination reveals normal growth and development, but he has chronic rhinitis and enlarged tonsils. Laboratory studies show normal total immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE), but markedly reduced levels of specific antibody responses to polysaccharide antigens (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae* type b) following vaccination. Flow cytometry analysis of his peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals normal numbers of CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, B cells, and NK cells. However, expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on activated CD4+ T cells is absent. His mother has a history of recurrent infections as a child but has since recovered.

(A) Deficiency in the complement component C3 would explain the recurrent bacterial infections due to impaired opsonization and bacterial lysis.
(B) A defect in the Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK) gene would lead to a failure of B cell development, resulting in panhypogammaglobulinemia and susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.
(C) The patient's inability to mount effective antibody responses to polysaccharide antigens, despite normal total immunoglobulin levels and T cell function, is most consistent with a defect in T cell-dependent B cell activation, specifically the interaction between CD40L on T cells and CD40 on B cells.
(D) A deficiency in the common gamma chain (γc) subunit, shared by receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21, would result in severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) characterized by absent T cells and NK cells, and severely impaired B cell function.
(E) A mutation in the gene encoding the catalytic subunit of phosphoinositide 3-kinase (PI3K), p110δ, would impair B cell receptor signaling and T cell activation, leading to impaired antibody production and T cell function.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections:** Suggests a defect in humoral immunity, particularly against encapsulated bacteria common in these sites.
*   **Onset at 6 months:** Timing is consistent with the waning of maternal antibodies, exposing an underlying immune defect.
*   **Normal total immunoglobulin levels:** Rules out primary antibody deficiencies like X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) or common variable immunodeficiency (CVID), which typically present with low IgG levels.
*   **Reduced specific antibody responses to polysaccharide antigens:** This is the key finding. Polysaccharide antigens are T-independent antigens, while protein antigens are T-dependent. The defect is specifically in the response to T-dependent antigens, which require T cell help for B cell activation, isotype switching (to IgG subclasses effective against encapsulated bacteria), and affinity maturation.
*   **Normal T cell numbers and function (except CD40L):** Rules out T cell deficiencies like DiGeorge syndrome or SCID.
*   **Absent CD40L expression on activated CD4+ T cells:** This is the specific molecular defect. CD40L (CD154) on activated T cells binds to CD40 on B cells, providing a crucial co-stimulatory signal (Signal 2) required for full B cell activation, proliferation, isotype switching (especially to IgG subclasses needed for opsonization), and affinity maturation in response to protein antigens.
*   **Mother's history:** Suggests an X-linked inheritance pattern, as CD40L deficiency (X-linked hyper-IgM syndrome) is inherited this way.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **T-dependent B cell activation:** Requires two signals. Signal 1 is antigen binding to the B cell receptor (BCR) and antigen processing/presentation on MHC class II. Signal 2 is co-stimulation, primarily provided by CD40L on activated T helper cells binding to CD40 on B cells. This interaction triggers intracellular signaling cascades (e.g., NF-κB activation) leading to B cell proliferation, differentiation into plasma cells and memory B cells, isotype switching (from IgM to IgG, IgA, IgE), and somatic hypermutation (affinity maturation).
*   **CD40L (CD154):** A type II transmembrane protein expressed on activated T cells, NK cells, and mast cells. It belongs to the TNF receptor superfamily. Its interaction with CD40 is essential for T cell-dependent immune responses.
*   **CD40:** A type I transmembrane protein expressed on B cells, macrophages, dendritic cells, and epithelial cells. It belongs to the TNF receptor superfamily.
*   **X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome:** Caused by mutations in the *CD40LG* gene (encoding CD40L) located on the X chromosome. Affected males present with recurrent infections, typically starting in infancy. They have normal or elevated IgM levels (as T-independent responses are intact) but very low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE due to the failure of isotype switching. They are susceptible to opportunistic infections like *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia and cryptosporidiosis, similar to T cell deficiencies, because CD40-CD40L interaction is also important for macrophage activation and T cell priming by dendritic cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Deficiency in C3:** C3 deficiency impairs the alternative and classical complement pathways, leading to defective opsonization (C3b) and bacterial lysis (MAC). While this causes recurrent bacterial infections, it doesn't explain the specific defect in antibody responses to polysaccharide antigens or the normal total immunoglobulin levels. C3 deficiency typically presents with low CH50 levels.
- (B): **Defect in BTK:** Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK) is essential for B cell development. Mutations cause X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients have very low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (panhypogammaglobulinemia) and absent B cells in the periphery. This contrasts with the patient's normal total Ig levels and normal B cell numbers.
- (D): **Deficiency in common gamma chain (γc):** The γc is part of receptors for several interleukins crucial for lymphocyte development and function (IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21). Deficiency leads to X-linked severe combined immunodeficiency (X-SCID), characterized by absent T cells and NK cells, and severely impaired B cell function. This patient has normal T cell numbers and function (except CD40L).
- (E): **Mutation in PI3Kδ:** Phosphoinositide 3-kinase delta (PI3Kδ) is involved in signaling downstream of both the B cell receptor (BCR) and the T cell receptor (TCR). Mutations cause Activated PI3Kδ Syndrome (APDS), characterized by recurrent infections, lymphoproliferation, and autoimmunity. While it affects both B and T cells, the specific defect in isotype switching and the normal total Ig levels with impaired polysaccharide response are more characteristic of CD40L deficiency. APDS typically involves elevated IgE and IgG4 levels.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | CD40L Deficiency (X-linked Hyper-IgM) | C3 Deficiency | BTK Deficiency (XLA) | γc Deficiency (X-SCID) | PI3Kδ Deficiency (APDS) |
| :-------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :------------ | :------------------- | :--------------------- | :---------------------- |
| **Inheritance**              | X-linked                             | Autosomal     | X-linked            | X-linked              | Autosomal Dominant      |
| **Total Ig Levels**          | Normal/High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE     | Normal        | Very Low (all)       | Very Low (all)        | Variable (often High IgE/IgG4) |
| **B Cell Numbers**           | Normal                               | Normal        | Absent              | Normal                | Normal                  |
| **T Cell Numbers**           | Normal                               | Normal        | Normal              | Absent                | Normal                  |
| **NK Cell Numbers**          | Normal                               | Normal        | Normal              | Absent                | Normal                  |
| **Response to Polysaccharide Ag** | Impaired (T-dependent)              | Normal        | Impaired            | Impaired              | Impaired                |
| **Response to Protein Ag**   | Impaired (T-dependent)              | Normal        | Impaired            | Impaired              | Impaired                |
| **Key Defect**               | CD40L expression                     | C3 protein    | BTK enzyme          | γc subunit            | PI3Kδ enzyme            |
| **Clinical Presentation**    | Recurrent infections, Opportunistic infections | Recurrent bacterial infections | Recurrent bacterial infections | Severe infections early in life | Recurrent infections, Lymphoproliferation, Autoimmunity |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CD40L on activated T cells provides essential co-stimulation (Signal 2) to B cells via CD40, crucial for T-dependent antibody responses, including isotype switching and affinity maturation. Deficiency leads to hyper-IgM syndrome, characterized by normal/high IgM but low IgG/IgA/IgE, and susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria and opportunistic infections. Differentiate this from other primary immunodeficiencies based on immunoglobulin levels, cell counts, and specific response patterns.

<a id='question-091'></a>

### Question 091: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 41 (antigenic)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ) in activating macrophages and the subsequent intracellular killing mechanisms employed by these activated macrophages against intracellular pathogens, specifically focusing on the role of reactive oxygen species (ROS) and reactive nitrogen species (RNO) production. (理解细胞因子（如 IFN-γ）在激活巨噬细胞中的作用，以及激活巨噬细胞对抗胞内病原体所采用的随后的细胞内杀伤机制，特别是活性氧 (ROS) 和活性氮 (RNO) 的产生。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of fever, night sweats, and a persistent, non-productive cough. He is a construction worker and reports recent exposure to bird droppings while working on an old building renovation project. Physical examination reveals bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy and scattered crackles in the lower lung fields. A chest X-ray shows diffuse interstitial infiltrates. Sputum samples are negative for acid-fast bacilli (AFB) on initial smear but positive on culture after 4 weeks. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis shows numerous macrophages containing small, round, acid-fast organisms within phagolysosomes. The patient is started on standard quadruple therapy for suspected tuberculosis. However, his symptoms worsen despite treatment, and he develops pancytopenia. Further investigation reveals markedly elevated levels of soluble IL-2 receptor (sIL-2R) and significantly decreased levels of IFN-γ in his serum. A biopsy of an enlarged lymph node shows non-caseating granulomas with numerous macrophages containing the same acid-fast organisms. Which of the following mechanisms is most likely impaired in this patient, leading to his inability to clear the infection despite standard therapy?

(A) Impaired phagocytosis of the organism by macrophages due to defects in complement receptor 3 (CR3).
(B) Defective production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) by macrophages due to impaired NADPH oxidase activity.
(C) Reduced expression of MHC class II molecules on macrophages, preventing effective T cell activation.
(D) Decreased production of IFN-γ by T helper 1 (Th1) cells, leading to inadequate macrophage activation.
(E) Impaired lysosomal fusion with phagosomes due to defects in Rab GTPases.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with fever, night sweats, cough, hilar lymphadenopathy, interstitial infiltrates – suggestive of an infectious process, potentially granulomatous, involving the lungs and lymph nodes.
- **Exposure History:** Construction worker, exposure to bird droppings – points towards potential exposure to *Chlamydia psittaci* (psittacosis) or *Histoplasma capsulatum* (histoplasmosis), both of which can cause granulomatous lung disease.
- **Diagnostic Findings:** Sputum culture positive for acid-fast bacilli (AFB) after 4 weeks – initially negative smear suggests low bacterial load or atypical staining. The organism is acid-fast, narrowing the possibilities (e.g., *Mycobacterium*, *Nocardia*). The presence of organisms within phagolysosomes in BAL macrophages indicates an intracellular pathogen.
- **Treatment Failure & Complications:** Worsening symptoms despite standard anti-TB therapy, development of pancytopenia – suggests the diagnosis is not typical TB or that the patient has an underlying immunodeficiency preventing effective clearance.
- **Immunological Findings:** Elevated sIL-2R and decreased IFN-γ – sIL-2R is a marker of T cell activation/proliferation, often elevated in infections and autoimmune diseases. Low IFN-γ is a critical clue, as IFN-γ is the primary cytokine responsible for activating macrophages to kill intracellular pathogens.
- **Histopathology:** Non-caseating granulomas with intracellular acid-fast organisms – non-caseating granulomas are characteristic of certain infections (e.g., *Mycobacterium avium complex*, *Nocardia*, fungal infections) and sarcoidosis, but are less typical for *M. tuberculosis* (which usually causes caseating granulomas). The presence of intracellular organisms confirms an intracellular pathogen.
- **Synthesis:** The clinical picture (granulomatous lung disease, intracellular acid-fast organism), treatment failure, and specific immunological findings (low IFN-γ) strongly suggest an underlying defect in cell-mediated immunity, specifically affecting Th1 responses and macrophage activation. The most likely diagnosis, given the exposure and clinical course, is an atypical mycobacterial infection (like *M. avium complex*) or potentially a fungal infection, exacerbated by impaired IFN-γ production. The question asks for the impaired mechanism leading to the inability to clear the infection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **IFN-γ and Macrophage Activation:** IFN-γ, produced primarily by Th1 cells and NK cells, is the key cytokine for activating macrophages. It binds to the IFN-γ receptor (IFNGR) on macrophages, triggering the JAK-STAT1 signaling pathway. This leads to the transcription of genes encoding proteins crucial for intracellular killing, including inducible nitric oxide synthase (iNOS) for producing reactive nitrogen species (RNO, like nitric oxide) and enzymes involved in the respiratory burst for producing reactive oxygen species (ROS).
- **Intracellular Killing Mechanisms:** Activated macrophages employ several mechanisms to kill intracellular pathogens:
    - **Respiratory Burst:** NADPH oxidase generates superoxide radicals (O2-), which are converted to other ROS like hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) and hypochlorous acid (HOCl).
    - **Nitric Oxide Production:** iNOS produces nitric oxide (NO), which combines with superoxide to form peroxynitrite (ONOO-), a potent RNO.
    - **Lysosomal Enzymes:** Enzymes like lysozyme, proteases, and lipases degrade pathogens within the phagolysosome.
    - **Nutrient Deprivation:** Macrophages sequester essential nutrients like iron and zinc, limiting pathogen growth.
- **Pathogen Evasion:** Intracellular pathogens often have mechanisms to evade macrophage killing, such as inhibiting phagosome-lysosome fusion (e.g., *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*, *Leishmania*), surviving within the phagolysosome (e.g., *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*), or inhibiting macrophage activation (e.g., some viruses, parasites).
- **In this Case:** The patient has low IFN-γ levels, indicating impaired Th1 response. This leads to inadequate activation of macrophages, rendering them less effective at killing the intracellular acid-fast organism via ROS and RNO production. The non-caseating granulomas suggest a less robust inflammatory response than typically seen with *M. tuberculosis*, consistent with impaired macrophage activation. The pancytopenia could be related to the underlying infection or potentially an associated immune dysregulation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Impaired phagocytosis of the organism by macrophages due to defects in complement receptor 3 (CR3). CR3 is involved in opsonization and phagocytosis, particularly for complement-coated particles and some bacteria. While phagocytosis is necessary, the vignette states the organism is *within* phagolysosomes, implying phagocytosis occurred. The primary defect suggested by low IFN-γ is *activation* of macrophages *after* phagocytosis, not the initial uptake. Defects in CR3 (e.g., Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency type 1) typically present earlier in life with recurrent bacterial infections and impaired wound healing.
- (B) Defective production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) by macrophages due to impaired NADPH oxidase activity. This describes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). CGD patients have recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms (like *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*) because macrophages cannot produce ROS. While ROS production is impaired in this patient due to lack of macrophage activation, the *primary* defect is the lack of IFN-γ signaling leading to activation, not a defect in the NADPH oxidase enzyme itself. CGD patients typically have normal IFN-γ levels and macrophage activation *in vitro* if stimulated appropriately.
- (C) Reduced expression of MHC class II molecules on macrophages, preventing effective T cell activation. Reduced MHC class II expression is seen in conditions like MHC class II deficiency or Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome, leading to impaired antigen presentation to CD4+ T cells and severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID)-like presentation. While T cell activation is crucial, the vignette points specifically to a defect in macrophage *activation* (via IFN-γ) rather than antigen presentation itself. The elevated sIL-2R suggests T cells are being activated, but the downstream effect on macrophages is impaired.
- (E) Impaired lysosomal fusion with phagosomes due to defects in Rab GTPases. This mechanism is used by pathogens like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* and *Salmonella* to survive within the phagosome. While some intracellular pathogens evade killing this way, the primary defect suggested by the low IFN-γ is the failure of macrophages to *activate* their killing mechanisms (ROS, RNO, lysosomal enzymes) *after* phagosome-lysosome fusion has occurred. Defects in Rab GTPases are not typically associated with low IFN-γ.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (D) - Impaired IFN-γ Production/Signaling | (A) Impaired Phagocytosis (e.g., CR3 defect) | (B) Impaired ROS (e.g., CGD) | (C) Impaired MHC II (e.g., MHC II Deficiency) | (E) Impaired Phagolysosome Fusion (e.g., M. tuberculosis) |
|-------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | ↓ IFN-γ → ↓ Macrophage Activation                      | Defective Phagocytosis                      | Defective NADPH Oxidase      | Defective Antigen Presentation                  | Pathogen Evasion of Fusion                            |
| **Key Cytokine**        | IFN-γ                                                    | N/A                                         | N/A                          | N/A                                             | N/A                                                      |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | Th1 cells, Macrophages                                   | Phagocytes (Macrophages, Neutrophils)        | Macrophages, Neutrophils     | Antigen-Presenting Cells (APCs)                | Macrophages                                              |
| **Killing Mechanism**   | ↓ ROS, ↓ RNO, ↓ Lysosomal Enzymes                       | Impaired Uptake                              | ↓ ROS                        | Impaired T cell help                             | Survival within phagosome                              |
| **Typical Infections**  | Intracellular pathogens (Mycobacteria, Fungi, Viruses)   | Catalase-positive bacteria, Fungi            | Catalase-positive organisms | Severe, broad infections (bacterial, viral)      | Specific pathogens (e.g., M. tuberculosis, Salmonella) |
| **Clinical Clues**      | Low IFN-γ, Granulomas, Intracellular organisms           | Recurrent bacterial infections, early onset  | Recurrent infections         | Severe immunodeficiency, early onset             | Specific pathogen, survival within phagosome             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is understanding the central role of IFN-γ in cell-mediated immunity, specifically its function in activating macrophages to kill intracellular pathogens via ROS and RNO production. Low IFN-γ levels point to a defect in Th1 responses. Differentiate this from defects in phagocytosis (CR3), ROS production (CGD), antigen presentation (MHC II deficiency), or pathogen evasion mechanisms (impaired phagolysosome fusion).

<a id='question-092'></a>

### Question 092: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 42 (Specificity)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The specificity of cell-mediated immunity relies on the T cell receptor (TCR) recognizing processed antigen presented by MHC molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs). This interaction, along with co-stimulatory signals, determines the activation and subsequent effector function of the T cell, ensuring targeted elimination of infected or abnormal cells. (细胞介导免疫的特异性依赖于T细胞受体 (TCR) 识别抗原呈递细胞 (APC) 呈递的加工后的抗原-MHC复合物。这种相互作用以及共刺激信号决定了T细胞的激活和随后的效应功能，从而确保对受感染或异常细胞的靶向清除。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of progressively worsening cough, fever, night sweats, and weight loss. He is a previously healthy software engineer with no significant past medical history, allergies, or recent travel. He denies smoking or illicit drug use. Physical examination reveals bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy and scattered crackles in the lower lung fields. A chest X-ray shows diffuse interstitial infiltrates. Sputum analysis reveals acid-fast bacilli (AFB). Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid is sent for culture and analysis. Flow cytometry of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a normal CD4/CD8 ratio. A skin test using purified protein derivative (PPD) is strongly positive (20 mm induration). The patient is started on a multi-drug anti-tuberculosis regimen. Two weeks later, he develops a severe rash, fever, and worsening liver function tests. Further investigation reveals elevated serum levels of TNF-alpha and IFN-gamma. Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the patient's initial immune response to *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by IgG antibodies binding to mycobacterial cell wall components.
(B) Complement-mediated lysis of infected macrophages by the classical pathway.
(C) Recognition of mycobacterial antigens presented by MHC class II molecules on infected macrophages by CD4+ T helper cells.
(D) Recognition of mycobacterial antigens presented by MHC class I molecules on infected macrophages by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
(E) Direct phagocytosis and killing of *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* by neutrophils via oxidative burst.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Young adult male with classic symptoms of pulmonary tuberculosis (cough, fever, night sweats, weight loss).
- **Physical Exam & Imaging:** Hilar lymphadenopathy and interstitial infiltrates on chest X-ray are consistent with TB.
- **Microbiology:** Sputum AFB positive confirms mycobacterial infection, likely *M. tuberculosis*.
- **Immunology:** Strongly positive PPD skin test indicates prior sensitization to *M. tuberculosis* antigens, suggesting a cell-mediated immune response. Normal CD4/CD8 ratio rules out primary immunodeficiency affecting T cell populations.
- **Clinical Course:** Development of a severe rash, fever, and liver dysfunction after starting anti-TB treatment suggests a drug reaction or, less commonly, Immune Reconstitution Inflammatory Syndrome (IRIS), although the elevated TNF-alpha and IFN-gamma point towards a robust, albeit potentially excessive, immune response.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of the *initial* immune response to *M. tuberculosis*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* is an intracellular pathogen that primarily infects macrophages.
- The host immune response to *M. tuberculosis* is predominantly cell-mediated.
- Macrophages phagocytose the bacilli but often fail to kill them due to the bacterium's ability to inhibit phagosome-lysosome fusion.
- Infected macrophages process mycobacterial antigens and present them on both MHC class I and MHC class II molecules.
- **MHC Class II Presentation:** This pathway presents exogenous antigens (like mycobacterial proteins taken up by the macrophage) to CD4+ T helper cells (Th cells). This is the primary pathway for initiating the adaptive immune response against intracellular pathogens residing within phagosomes. Activated CD4+ Th1 cells secrete cytokines like IFN-gamma, which activates macrophages to become more effective killers (e.g., via increased production of reactive oxygen species and nitric oxide) and promotes granuloma formation to contain the infection.
- **MHC Class I Presentation:** This pathway typically presents endogenous antigens (like viral proteins synthesized within the cell). However, *M. tuberculosis* can also access the cytosol and be presented on MHC class I molecules, allowing recognition by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). CTLs can kill infected cells, but their role in TB immunity is considered secondary to that of CD4+ Th1 cells.
- **Antibodies:** While antibodies (IgG, IgM) are produced against *M. tuberculosis*, they play a limited role in controlling the infection because the bacteria are primarily intracellular. ADCC (Option A) is not a major mechanism against intracellular pathogens like *M. tuberculosis*.
- **Complement:** Complement activation (Option B) is generally ineffective against intracellular bacteria residing within macrophages.
- **Neutrophils:** Neutrophils (Option E) are recruited to sites of infection but are less effective than macrophages at controlling *M. tuberculosis* due to the bacterium's resistance mechanisms and intracellular location.
- **PPD Skin Test:** The positive PPD test indicates sensitization of T cells (primarily CD4+ Th1 cells) to mycobacterial antigens. This memory response is characteristic of cell-mediated immunity.
- **Elevated Cytokines:** High levels of TNF-alpha and IFN-gamma are hallmarks of the Th1 response activated against *M. tuberculosis*.

Therefore, the initial and primary adaptive immune response involves CD4+ T helper cells recognizing mycobacterial antigens presented on MHC class II molecules by infected macrophages.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC)**: ADCC involves NK cells or other cytotoxic cells recognizing antibody-coated target cells. While antibodies are produced against *M. tuberculosis*, they are not the primary mechanism for controlling this intracellular pathogen, and ADCC is not a significant factor in TB immunity. The main defense is cell-mediated.
- (B) **Complement-mediated lysis**: Complement is effective against extracellular bacteria or bacteria encapsulated in complement-fixing antibodies. *M. tuberculosis* is primarily intracellular, making complement-mediated lysis an ineffective mechanism.
- (D) **Recognition of mycobacterial antigens presented by MHC class I molecules on infected macrophages by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs)**: While MHC class I presentation does occur (cross-presentation), the primary T cell response driving immunity to *M. tuberculosis* is mediated by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing antigens presented on MHC class II molecules. CD8+ T cells play a less prominent role. The question asks for the *best* explanation of the *initial* response, which is predominantly CD4+ Th1 mediated via MHC II.
- (E) **Direct phagocytosis and killing of *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* by neutrophils via oxidative burst**: Neutrophils are important for extracellular bacterial infections. *M. tuberculosis* is primarily an intracellular pathogen residing within macrophages. While neutrophils are recruited, they are less effective than activated macrophages in controlling TB. Macrophages are the primary phagocytes involved, and the key immune response is T cell-mediated activation of these macrophages.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* Immunity | Antibody-Mediated Immunity (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*) | Complement-Mediated Immunity (e.g., *Neisseria meningitidis*) | Neutrophil-Mediated Immunity (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Immune Cell** | CD4+ T helper cells (Th1)              | B cells, Plasma cells                                           | Complement proteins                                           | Neutrophils                                                 |
| **Key Mechanism**       | Cell-mediated (MHC II presentation)    | Antibody production, Opsonization, Neutralization, ADCC         | Lysis via MAC, Opsonization (C3b)                             | Phagocytosis, Oxidative burst, NETosis                       |
| **Pathogen Location**   | Intracellular (macrophages)            | Extracellular                                                  | Extracellular                                                 | Extracellular/Intracellular (phagocytosed)                   |
| **Role of Antibodies**  | Limited                               | Major role                                                     | Minor role (opsonization)                                     | Minor role (opsonization)                                   |
| **Role of Complement**  | Limited                               | Minor role                                                     | Major role                                                    | Minor role                                                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary adaptive immune response against intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* is cell-mediated, involving CD4+ T helper cells recognizing antigens presented on MHC class II molecules by infected macrophages. This contrasts with humoral immunity (antibodies) effective against extracellular pathogens or complement-mediated lysis. Remember that intracellular pathogens often evade antibody and complement-mediated defenses. Key differentials include extracellular bacterial infections (e.g., *S. pneumoniae*, *N. meningitidis*) where antibodies and complement are crucial, and infections primarily controlled by neutrophils (e.g., *S. aureus*).

<a id='question-093'></a>

### Question 093: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 43 (response.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) in recognizing and eliminating intracellular pathogens, specifically viruses, and the mechanism of target cell killing via perforin/granzyme pathway and Fas/FasL pathway. (理解CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞(CTLs)在识别和清除细胞内病原体，特别是病毒中的作用，以及通过穿孔素/粒酶途径和Fas/FasL途径杀死靶细胞的机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, myalgia, and a diffuse maculopapular rash. He reports recent travel to Southeast Asia for a business trip, where he engaged in unprotected sexual intercourse. Physical examination reveals generalized lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory tests show elevated liver enzymes (AST, ALT) and a positive HIV antibody test. CD4+ T cell count is 450 cells/µL, and the viral load is high. The patient is started on antiretroviral therapy. Six months later, despite adherence to therapy, he develops persistent generalized lymphadenopathy and a new skin lesion on his arm, which is biopsied. Histopathology of the lymph node and skin lesion shows atypical lymphoid cells with large nuclei and prominent nucleoli, infiltrating the tissue. These cells are positive for CD3 and CD8 but negative for CD4. Flow cytometry reveals a clonal population of these CD8+ T cells. Further investigation reveals that these atypical CD8+ T cells express high levels of Fas ligand (FasL).

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the destruction of target cells by the atypical CD8+ T cells in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing Fc receptors on infected cells.
(B) Release of perforin and granzymes by the atypical CD8+ T cells, leading to apoptosis of target cells.
(C) Binding of FasL expressed on the atypical CD8+ T cells to Fas receptors on target cells, inducing apoptosis.
(D) Phagocytosis of infected cells by macrophages activated by cytokines released from CD4+ helper T cells.
(E) Complement-mediated lysis of target cells due to activation of the classical pathway by antibodies bound to viral antigens.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with HIV infection (positive antibody test, low-normal CD4 count, high viral load) presenting with symptoms suggestive of acute retroviral syndrome (fever, myalgia, rash, lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly).
- **Timeline:** Patient is on ART for 6 months.
- **New Development:** Persistent lymphadenopathy and a new skin lesion develop despite ART.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Histopathology shows atypical lymphoid cells (large nuclei, prominent nucleoli) infiltrating lymph node and skin.
- **Immunophenotyping:** These cells are CD3+ and CD8+ but CD4-. They are clonal and express high levels of FasL.
- **Diagnosis Implied:** The clinical picture (HIV infection, atypical CD8+ T cell proliferation, lymphadenopathy, skin lesions) strongly suggests the development of a T-cell lymphoma, specifically a type associated with HIV, such as Adult T-cell leukemia/lymphoma (ATLL) or a related entity. The key feature is the atypical CD8+ T cells expressing FasL.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism by which these atypical CD8+ T cells destroy target cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs):** These cells are crucial for cell-mediated immunity against intracellular pathogens (like viruses) and tumor cells. They recognize peptide antigens presented on MHC class I molecules on the surface of target cells.
- **CTL Killing Mechanisms:** CTLs employ two primary mechanisms to induce apoptosis in target cells:
    1.  **Perforin/Granzyme Pathway:** CTLs release perforin, which polymerizes to form pores in the target cell membrane. Granzymes (serine proteases) enter through these pores and activate caspases within the target cell, triggering apoptosis.
    2.  **Fas/FasL Pathway:** CTLs express Fas ligand (FasL, also known as CD95L) on their surface. FasL binds to its receptor, Fas (also known as CD95 or APO-1), which is expressed on many cell types, including target cells. This interaction triggers the formation of the death-inducing signaling complex (DISC) within the target cell, activating caspases and inducing apoptosis.
- **HIV and T-cell Lymphomas:** HIV infection is associated with an increased risk of certain lymphomas, particularly T-cell lymphomas. The chronic immune activation and viral persistence can lead to clonal expansion of T cells, sometimes resulting in malignant transformation. The atypical CD8+ T cells described in the vignette, expressing high levels of FasL, are characteristic of certain aggressive T-cell lymphomas associated with HIV. These malignant cells utilize the Fas/FasL pathway to kill surrounding normal cells or other immune cells, contributing to the pathology.
- **Focus 43 (response):** This refers to the mechanisms of cell-mediated immunity, specifically the effector functions of CTLs. The vignette directly tests the understanding of the Fas/FasL pathway as a mechanism of CTL-mediated cytotoxicity.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC)** is mediated by NK cells (or other cytotoxic cells like macrophages, neutrophils, eosinophils) that recognize antibody-coated target cells via Fc receptors. This mechanism involves antibodies binding to antigens on the target cell surface, followed by NK cell binding and release of cytotoxic granules. This is not the primary mechanism described for the atypical CD8+ T cells in the vignette, which are directly interacting with target cells via FasL.
- (B): **Release of perforin and granzymes** is a major mechanism of CTL-mediated killing. While CTLs (including the atypical ones) can use this pathway, the vignette specifically highlights the high expression of FasL on these atypical cells, suggesting the Fas/FasL pathway is particularly relevant or dominant in this context. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism given the specific finding of high FasL expression.
- (D): **Phagocytosis by macrophages** is a mechanism for clearing infected or apoptotic cells, often mediated by CD4+ helper T cells activating macrophages via cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ). This is not a direct killing mechanism employed by CD8+ CTLs themselves.
- (E): **Complement-mediated lysis** involves activation of the complement cascade, leading to the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) which punches holes in the target cell membrane. This is typically triggered by antibodies (classical pathway) or directly by microbial surfaces (alternative pathway). It is not a mechanism used by CTLs.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - Fas/FasL Pathway | Distractor (A) - ADCC | Distractor (B) - Perforin/Granzyme | Distractor (D) - Macrophage Phagocytosis | Distractor (E) - Complement Lysis |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Mediator Cell**       | CD8+ CTL (atypical)                    | NK cell              | CD8+ CTL                          | Macrophage                               | Complement proteins               |
| **Target Recognition**  | Fas receptor on target cell            | Fc receptor on NK cell | MHC-I/peptide on target cell      | Opsonins/Cytokines on target cell        | Antibody/C3b on target cell       |
| **Mechanism**           | FasL-Fas interaction -> Apoptosis      | Cytotoxic granules  | Perforin/Granzyme entry -> Apoptosis | Engulfment & degradation                 | MAC formation -> Lysis            |
| **Key Molecules**       | FasL, Fas                               | Fc receptors, Abs    | Perforin, Granzymes                | Cytokines (IFN-γ), Opsonins              | Antibodies, C3b, MAC              |
| **Relevance to Vignette** | High FasL expression on atypical cells | Not described       | Possible, but FasL highlighted      | Not direct CTL mechanism                 | Not direct CTL mechanism           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) kill target cells primarily via the perforin/granzyme pathway and the Fas/FasL pathway, both leading to apoptosis. The Fas/FasL pathway involves direct cell-cell contact where FasL on the CTL binds to Fas on the target cell. Understanding these mechanisms is crucial for comprehending cell-mediated immunity against viruses and tumors. Differentiating CTL killing mechanisms from other cytotoxic pathways like ADCC, macrophage phagocytosis, and complement lysis is essential. HIV-associated lymphomas can involve atypical T cells with altered expression of cytotoxic molecules like FasL.

<a id='question-094'></a>

### Question 094: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 2 Focus 44 (Stimulatory)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of Toll-like receptors (TLRs) in innate immunity, specifically TLR4 activation by lipopolysaccharide (LPS) leading to the activation of NF-κB and subsequent inflammatory cytokine production, which is crucial for initiating adaptive immune responses and host defense against Gram-negative bacteria. (理解 Toll 样受体 (TLRs) 在先天免疫中的作用，特别是 TLR4 被脂多糖 (LPS) 激活，导致 NF-κB 激活和随后的炎症细胞因子产生，这对于启动适应性免疫反应和宿主对革兰氏阴性菌的防御至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) and poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of worsening shortness of breath, productive cough with purulent sputum, and fever (38.5°C). He reports recent travel to a rural area where he was exposed to bird droppings while cleaning out an old barn. Physical examination reveals tachypnea (28 breaths/min), tachycardia (110 bpm), and diffuse crackles on auscultation. Arterial blood gas analysis shows hypoxemia (PaO2 60 mmHg) and hypercapnia (PaCO2 55 mmHg). A chest X-ray demonstrates bilateral patchy infiltrates. Sputum Gram stain reveals numerous Gram-negative rods. The patient is started on empiric antibiotics, but his condition deteriorates rapidly, progressing to septic shock characterized by hypotension (systolic BP 80 mmHg) and disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC). Blood cultures grow *Klebsiella pneumoniae*. Subsequent investigation reveals that the patient's neutrophils exhibit a markedly reduced response to LPS stimulation *in vitro*, failing to upregulate CD69 and produce TNF-α.

Which of the following intracellular signaling pathways is most likely impaired in this patient, contributing to his severe susceptibility to *Klebsiella pneumoniae* infection?

(A) MyD88-dependent pathway involving IRAK kinases
(B) TRIF-dependent pathway involving TRAF3 and IRF3
(C) NOD-like receptor (NLR) pathway involving ASC and caspase-1
(D) RIG-I-like receptor (RLR) pathway involving MAVS and IRF7
(E) Complement receptor signaling pathway involving Syk kinase

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with COPD and poorly controlled diabetes. These comorbidities increase susceptibility to infections, particularly respiratory infections.
*   **Exposure:** Exposure to bird droppings in a rural area suggests potential exposure to environmental pathogens, including Gram-negative bacteria like *Klebsiella pneumoniae*.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Acute respiratory distress, fever, purulent sputum, hypoxemia, hypercapnia, bilateral infiltrates – consistent with severe pneumonia/sepsis.
*   **Microbiology:** Sputum Gram stain shows Gram-negative rods. Blood cultures grow *Klebsiella pneumoniae*, a common cause of severe pneumonia, especially in immunocompromised or comorbid patients.
*   **Clinical Course:** Rapid deterioration to septic shock and DIC indicates a severe systemic inflammatory response, often associated with Gram-negative sepsis.
*   **Immunological Finding:** Neutrophils exhibit a markedly reduced response to LPS stimulation *in vitro*, failing to upregulate CD69 (an early activation marker) and produce TNF-α (a key pro-inflammatory cytokine). This points to a defect in the innate immune response to LPS, a major component of the outer membrane of Gram-negative bacteria like *Klebsiella pneumoniae*.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the impaired intracellular signaling pathway responsible for the defective response to LPS.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **LPS Recognition:** Lipopolysaccharide (LPS), also known as endotoxin, is a potent immunostimulant found in the outer membrane of Gram-negative bacteria. It is recognized by Toll-like receptor 4 (TLR4) on the surface of immune cells, primarily macrophages and dendritic cells, but also neutrophils.
*   **TLR4 Signaling Pathways:** TLR4 signaling involves two main adaptor proteins:
    *   **MyD88:** Recruitment of MyD88 to TLR4 leads to the activation of the interleukin-1 receptor-associated kinase (IRAK) family (IRAK1, IRAK4). Activated IRAKs recruit TNF receptor-associated factor 6 (TRAF6), which activates the IκB kinase (IKK) complex. IKK phosphorylates IκB, leading to its degradation and the release of the transcription factor NF-κB. NF-κB translocates to the nucleus and induces the transcription of genes encoding pro-inflammatory cytokines like TNF-α, IL-1β, and IL-6. This pathway is crucial for initiating the inflammatory response to Gram-negative infections.
    *   **TRIF:** Recruitment of TRIF (TIR-domain-containing adapter-inducing interferon-β) leads to the activation of TRAF3 and the kinases TBK1 and IKKε. This pathway primarily induces the production of type I interferons (IFN-β) via the activation of interferon regulatory factor 3 (IRF3) and NF-κB-dependent cytokine production.
*   **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's neutrophils fail to respond to LPS stimulation by upregulating CD69 and producing TNF-α. CD69 is an early activation marker, and TNF-α is a key cytokine produced downstream of TLR4 signaling via the MyD88 pathway. This specific defect points to an impairment in the MyD88-dependent signaling cascade.
*   **MyD88 Deficiency:** Mutations in the *MYD88* gene cause autosomal recessive MyD88 deficiency, characterized by severe susceptibility to recurrent pyogenic bacterial infections, particularly those caused by Gram-positive cocci (like *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Streptococcus pneumoniae*) and Gram-negative bacteria. Patients often present with severe sepsis and failure to thrive. The impaired response to LPS stimulation is a hallmark of this condition.
*   **Other TLR Pathways:**
    *   TRIF pathway (Option B) is involved in type I interferon production, which is important for antiviral responses but also contributes to bacterial clearance. However, the primary defect described (lack of TNF-α production) is more strongly associated with the MyD88 pathway.
    *   NLR pathway (Option C) recognizes intracellular bacterial components (e.g., peptidoglycan, flagellin) and is involved in inflammasome activation and IL-1β processing.
    *   RLR pathway (Option D) recognizes viral RNA and is crucial for antiviral immunity.
    *   Complement receptor signaling (Option E) involves receptors for complement fragments (e.g., C3b, C5a) and is important for opsonization, phagocytosis, and inflammation, but it is distinct from the direct LPS-TLR4 pathway.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **MyD88-dependent pathway involving IRAK kinases:** This is the correct answer. LPS binding to TLR4 recruits MyD88, initiating a cascade involving IRAK kinases, TRAF6, IKK, and NF-κB, leading to TNF-α production. The patient's neutrophils fail to produce TNF-α in response to LPS, indicating a defect in this pathway.
- (B) **TRIF-dependent pathway involving TRAF3 and IRF3:** This pathway is also activated by TLR4 but primarily leads to type I interferon production. While important for immunity, the lack of TNF-α production is more characteristic of a MyD88 defect. Patients with TRIF deficiency typically have impaired antiviral responses but less severe bacterial susceptibility compared to MyD88 deficiency.
- (C) **NOD-like receptor (NLR) pathway involving ASC and caspase-1:** NLRs recognize intracellular bacterial components and activate the inflammasome, leading to caspase-1 activation and IL-1β/IL-18 processing. This pathway is not directly involved in the initial response to extracellular LPS via TLR4.
- (D) **RIG-I-like receptor (RLR) pathway involving MAVS and IRF7:** RLRs recognize viral RNA in the cytoplasm and activate MAVS, leading to the activation of IRF7 and NF-κB, primarily inducing type I interferon production. This pathway is involved in antiviral immunity, not the response to bacterial LPS.
- (E) **Complement receptor signaling pathway involving Syk kinase:** Complement receptors bind complement fragments (e.g., C3b, C5a) and signal through Syk kinase, enhancing phagocytosis and inflammation. This pathway is distinct from the TLR4-MyD88 pathway activated by LPS.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | TLR4/MyD88 Pathway (Correct) | TLR4/TRIF Pathway | NLR Pathway | RLR Pathway | Complement Pathway |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------- | :---------------- | :---------- | :---------- | :----------------- |
| **Ligand**              | LPS                          | LPS               | Intracellular Bacteria | Viral RNA | Complement Fragments |
| **Receptor**            | TLR4                         | TLR4              | NLRs        | RLRs       | Complement Receptors |
| **Adaptor Protein**     | MyD88                        | TRIF              | ASC         | MAVS       | N/A                |
| **Key Kinases**         | IRAK                         | TRAF3, TBK1, IKKε | Caspase-1  | N/A        | Syk                |
| **Key Transcription Factors** | NF-κB                        | IRF3, NF-κB       | N/A         | IRF7, NF-κB | N/A                |
| **Primary Cytokines**   | TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6           | IFN-β, TNF-α, IL-6 | IL-1β, IL-18 | IFN-β      | N/A                |
| **Main Function**       | Bacterial Sepsis Response    | Antiviral Response | Inflammasome | Antiviral Response | Opsonization, Phagocytosis |
| **Deficiency Consequence** | Severe Bacterial Sepsis     | Impaired Antiviral | Autoinflammatory Diseases | Impaired Antiviral | Increased Bacterial Infections |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary signaling pathway for LPS recognition via TLR4, leading to the production of key pro-inflammatory cytokines like TNF-α, involves the MyD88 adaptor protein and downstream kinases like IRAK. Defects in this pathway, such as MyD88 deficiency, result in severe susceptibility to bacterial infections, particularly Gram-negative sepsis. Differentiating TLR4 signaling pathways (MyD88 vs. TRIF) is crucial, as TRIF primarily mediates type I interferon production. Remember that TLR4 can signal through both MyD88 and TRIF, but the lack of TNF-α points specifically to a MyD88 defect.
